# Ordered Shared-Patterns Implementation Plan

## Summary

This plan is derived from `shared-patterns-findings.md`, manual review of both consolidation-error rows, and spot-checks against source under `src/`. False-positive, one-copy, generated, and behavior-incompatible candidates are excluded.

| Metric | Count | Basis |
|---|---:|---|
| Use-existing fixes | 155 | Consolidated `replace-with-existing` category count |
| New shared modules/groups | 14 | Near-duplicate families retained after merging and behavior validation |
| Call sites to change | 1348 unique source files | File references in consolidated findings; one file may contain several changed call sites |
| Likely real bugs | 532 | Deduplicated bullets under `Possible real bugs`; category count is 541 including recognized bug subcategories |

## Consolidation errors: manual inclusion decision

| Source row | Decision | Why | Implementation action |
|---|---|---|---|
| `findings/chunk-0434.md:3` | **Include** | Source spot-check confirms place-first plus finite 0.01-degree geo-cell identity in charge interruption, charger health, and charger resilience. Resilience alone deliberately adds charger-type/unknown fallback. | Add a normalized place/geo primitive; keep fallback policy caller-owned. |
| `findings/chunk-0434.md:4` | **Include** | Source spot-check confirms equivalent regularized incomplete-beta continued-fraction/CDF implementations with argument-order differences. | Add a canonical statistics helper and adapt both callers; pin numerical edge cases with shared tests. |

## Work items (implementation order)

## 1. Canonical statistics primitives

**Goal:** Create `src/web/src/lib/statistics.ts` with `regularizedIncompleteBeta(x,a,b)` and `betaQuantile(p,a,b)`.

**Existing pattern or new module:** Create `src/web/src/lib/statistics

**Call sites:** `src/web/src/features/charging/lib/chargeInterruption.ts:180-254`; `src/web/src/features/analytics/lib/firmwareImpact.ts:158-175`

**Behavior differences the shared version must support explicitly:** The existing analytics implementation uses `(a,b,x)` while the interruption implementation uses `(x,a,b)`. Preserve caller-specific outputs, but centralize CDF and inverse behavior. Reject or define finite/non-finite handling, x boundaries, shape parameters, and numerical convergence consistently.

**Tests needed:** Known textbook beta CDF values, symmetry, endpoints, invalid a/b/x, Infinity/NaN, singleton/degenerate posterior parameters, and cross-caller parity. No timezone/locale behavior applies.

**Risk:** Medium

**Suggested guard:** Golden numerical vectors and grep guard against second local incomplete-beta implementations.

## Likely real bugs found

- **chunk-0004.md:5** — `cmd/automation-worker/main.go:51-59`: The built-in `healthcheck` mode uses an unbounded `http.Get` and never closes the successful response body. Unlike the bounded, context-aware healthcheck in `cmd/export-worker/main.go:43-47` and `cmd/export-worker/main.go:270-292`, this probe can hang indefinitely if loopback connectivity stalls, and the response body is left open. The same unbounded/no-close pattern also appears in `cmd/notification-worker/main.go:92-99` and `cmd/teslasync/main.go:74-80`; `healthcheckExitCode` is the existing robust implementation to reuse.
- **chunk-0005.md:5** — `cmd/backup-restore-drill/main.go:106-118`: `parseCriticalTables` treats an empty/whitespace value as three defaults, but a value containing only separators (for example `,,,` or `", , "`) produces an empty slice. Passing that slice as `criticalTables` to `backuprestore.Restorer.Run` skips its post-import `COUNT(*)` verification loop entirely, while it is passed to `backupverify.NewVerifier`, whose empty-list constructor fallback changes it to only `vehicles`; consequently separator-only configuration can falsely degrade coverage without failing the drill. The neighboring parser in `cmd/backup-verify/main.go:111-123` demonstrates the same CSV filtering pattern but preserves explicit nil/non-nil behavior; separator-only configuration should be normalized to the drill's three-table default consistently rather than yielding no restorer checks.
- **chunk-0008.md:5** — `src/cmd/export-worker/main.go:127-177`; `src/internal/backup/processor.go:67-186`; `src/internal/database/backup/config_repo.go:145-150`: The scheduled-backup ticker finds due configs, but does not advance or claim `next_run_at` before launching the run. Instead, `BackupConfigRepo.MarkRun` is called only by `Processor.RunBackup` after reaching the end of a successful or partial execution path. Early failures (run still marked running at `processor.go:67`, then marshal/compress/provider/upload failure at `processor.go:90-126`) return before `MarkRun`, so the config remains due. The same scheduler tick also has no status/config claim, so multiple workers, ticks, or replicas can enqueue the same config while one run is still running. Scheduled backups can be duplicated repeatedly every 60 seconds when a run fails before completion, and can overlap under concurrent export workers; postpone/claim `next_run_at` atomically when the config is dequeued, and ensure a clear claim exists before starting the run. Reconcile failed/retried runs without keeping `GetDueConfigs` permanently eligible.
- **chunk-0009.md:3** — `src/cmd/fleet-config-validator/main.go:238-242`; `src/cmd/fleet-config-validator/main_test.go:63-71`: `brokerHostPortRE` accepts syntactically formed but invalid ports, including `mosquitto:0` and `mosquitto:65536`, because it checks only 1-5 digits and never parses the port. The CLI therefore reports success for a broker configuration the MQTT endpoint cannot use. Port range validation and test coverage should be added.
- **chunk-0009.md:4** — `src/cmd/fleet-config-validator/main.go:219-229`: `--check-paths` silently ignores every `os.Stat` error other than `os.ErrNotExist`; permission/IO failures consequently look like successful checks. A directory at a cert/key path is also accepted as existing. This makes the option's existence guarantee unreliable on shared CI or container filesystems.
- **chunk-0011.md:3** — src/cmd/notification-worker/main.go:92-98; src/cmd/notification-worker/main.go:408-418; src/cmd/automation-worker/main.go:54-58; src/cmd/export-worker/main.go:271-290: The notification-worker built-in `healthcheck` probes hardcode port `8081`, but its server honors `HEALTH_PORT` (and falls back to `8081`). When the deployment sets a custom `HEALTH_PORT`, the health endpoint starts on the custom port while the probe still requests `8081`, causing probes and container healthchecks to fail despite a healthy worker.
- **chunk-0012.md:3** — `src/cmd/ocpp-server/main.go:186-202`; `src/internal/ocpp/server.go:91-125`: `run` calls `http.Server.Shutdown` on cancellation and returns immediately, but WebSocket connections are hijacked and are not tracked or closed by `http.Server.Shutdown`. The OCPP read loop is blocked in `ReadMessage`; it watches only its read deadline and never observes the run context, so a graceful shutdown can return with charger connections still live and later dispatch through a session store already closed in `main` (after `run` returns). Establish explicit OCPP connection ownership and close/cancel all hijacked WebSocket connections on shutdown, or pass shutdown-context-aware cancellation into `Server.serve` and track connections there before `run` returns.
- **chunk-0012.md:4** — `src/cmd/ocpp-server/main.go:188-202`: The `select` in `run` can choose the context-cancellation branch while the serve goroutine is simultaneously reporting an unexpected `Serve` error. That error remains unread and `run` proceeds directly to `Shutdown`, which can return nil, converting the serve failure into a clean exit instead of `serve: ...`. On cancellation, after stopping or shutting down, drain or otherwise prioritize the `serveErr` result; do not return nil unless `Serve` reported `http.ErrServerClosed` (or `Shutdown` itself is the sole clean-shutdown cause).
- **chunk-0015.md:3** — `src/cmd/pub-test-signal/main.go:442-456` and `src/cmd/pub-test-signal/main.go:323`: `pairLatLonRows` increments `stats.datumsEncoded` once for a synthetic `Location` row, then the main replay loop increments the same counter again when that row is published. The CSV replay summary therefore reports two encoded datums for each successfully published latitude/longitude pair; it also makes the encoded/skip totals inconsistent with the actual CSV rows. Single-copy edge in replay accounting: pre-pass mutation at the first location is counted, while the second copy at publish time counts the same synthetic datum again.
- **chunk-0018.md:3** — `src/cmd/resubscribe/main.go:414-420` and `src/internal/api/devtools/handler.go:734-748`: The resubscribe worker payload omits the `ca` field required to configure vehicles to trust the telemetry server's TLS certificate. The equivalent devtools subscription path supplies the default Let's Encrypt root CA (or caller-provided PEM), while the worker sends only hostname, port, and fields, so Tesla can reject or fail to activate the replacement subscription despite the worker reporting a successful HTTP status. The devtools implementation at `handler.go:734-748` explicitly treats the trusted-root CA as required for Fleet Telemetry; `main.go` has no `CA` assignment in `FleetTelemetryConfigPayload`, creating a configuration contract mismatch that only affects real (non-dry-run) operator resubscribes.
- **chunk-0019.md:3** — `src/cmd/rollback-evaluator/main.go:116-149` and `src/cmd/rollback-evaluator/main.go:232-256`: Live evaluation can anchor the entire Prometheus range window to an old `observed_at` copied from the deploy snapshot. In live mode, `snap.ObservedAt` should represent current wall-clock evaluation time; instead, a non-zero timestamp from the snapshot replaces it before lookback is computed and live points are merged, causing queries to scrape stale deployment-era history and possibly suppress stale-data detection. The snapshot merge marks deploy observations authoritative but mistakenly applies that timestamp to live measurements too. A snapshot generated before the bake window can make newly returned range points appear fresh relative to `snap.ObservedAt`, even though they are far behind actual time.
- **chunk-0019.md:4** — `src/cmd/rollback-evaluator/main.go:347-366`: The Prometheus range parser filters `NaN` but not positive or negative infinity. `strconv.ParseFloat` accepts `+Inf`, `-Inf`, and `Inf`, so a malformed/degenerate series value is appended as an observation and can affect warn/rollback or `lt` comparisons instead of being treated as missing. This is an edge beyond the existing NaN handling: `Inf` is a numeric result from `ParseFloat` but is not a usable metric sample; `+Inf` can trigger `gt`/`gte` thresholds and `-Inf` can trigger `lt`/`lte` thresholds, with stale/missing telemetry incorrectly becoming a health verdict.
- **chunk-0021.md:5** — `src/cmd/slo-coverage-audit/main.go:45`, `src/cmd/slo-coverage-audit/main.go:138-151`, `src/internal/api/router.go:3210`, `src/internal/api/router.go:3362-3370`, `src/internal/api/router.go:3506-3514`, `src/internal/api/router.go:4929`: The SLO audit does not compose Chi routes; `loadRoutes` only regexp-extracts leaf handler literals. It ignores every `r.Route` prefix and returns fragments such as `/state`, `/`, `/live`, or `/history` instead of the effective full paths such as `/api/v1/vehicles/{vehicleID}/state` or `/api/v1/signals/{vehicleID}/live`. This also means the audit cannot validate effective nested routes or per-route catalog matches. Cataloged route SLOs use full effective paths at `src/slo/catalog.yaml:120-121` (and elsewhere), while the router declares those same paths through nested prefixes in `src/internal/api/router.go`; the extractor never joins the two representations, so a catalog mismatch is hidden and the report compares unrelated route strings.
- **chunk-0021.md:6** — `src/cmd/slo-coverage-audit/main.go:159-169`, `src/slo/catalog.yaml:20-21`, `src/slo/catalog.yaml:110-111`: `loadCatalogCoverage` defines transitive coverage merely by seeing the availability metric anywhere in the catalog. The two per-route SLO expressions at `src/slo/catalog.yaml:20-21` also reference `teslasync_red_http_requests_total`, but `strings.Contains` cannot distinguish route-filtered availability counters or latency histograms from an unfiltered global availability SLI. An SLO catalog containing only route-filtered uses of the counter would incorrectly set `transitive=true`, granting every user-facing route global coverage and defeating the audit. The intended global exception must detect an availability SLI whose metric use has no route filter, as described by the function comments at `src/cmd/slo-coverage-audit/main.go:18` and `src/cmd/slo-coverage-audit/main.go:159-169`.
- **chunk-0022.md:5** — `src/cmd/slogen/generate_dashboards.go:112-145`: `runGenerateDashboards` writes current SLO dashboards but never removes dashboards for SLO names that have been deleted from the catalog. Normal generation leaves obsolete `slo-<name>.json` files in place, and `--check` reports only those expected files and also does not detect extra stale files. Provisioning directories can therefore continue serving a removed SLO dashboard after catalog removal. Removed catalog entries leave their previously generated `slo-*` JSON file as a second live artifact; stale generated artifacts bypass the check because no directory reconciliation occurs in either mode.
- **chunk-0022.md:6** — `src/cmd/slogen/generate_dashboards.go:265-280` and `src/cmd/slogen/generate_dashboards.go:89-105`: The per-SLO error-budget panel is hard-coded to 30 days (`Error budget remaining (30d)` and `ratio_rate30d`) even though the catalog declares each SLO's own `Window` (for example 7d or 28d). A 7-day or 28-day objective therefore shows and computes against the 30-day recording series instead of its nominal objective window, misreporting remaining budget and making the dashboard disagree with runtime nominal-window snapshots and the tracker. Recording rules always include `ratio_rate30d`, but the dashboard's `s.Window` field is never used here; objective-window semantics diverge in the same generated dashboard across SLOs with 7d/28d catalogs.
- **chunk-0023.md:3** — `src/internal/ops/smoke.go:65-67` and `src/internal/ops/smoke.go:145-148` and `src/internal/ops/smokerun.go:162-163`: Smoke-path safety validation compares the manifest string literally against `DrainPath`, while request execution appends that same raw string to the deployment base URL. A manifest such as `/internal/flush?probe=1` bypasses the exact-key guard, because the query does not change the HTTP path; the runner still sends it to the pod-fatal drain handler. The forbidden-path check is exact-string based at validation, but the runner performs URL execution on the raw path/query copy; equivalent drain URLs can therefore be accepted and mutate a live deployment.
- **chunk-0025.md:5** — `src/cmd/trace-coverage-audit/main.go:223-252`: The audit's report path is resolved from the process working directory even when `-root` points at another repository tree. `run` passes the unqualified `reportPath` to `writeReport`, while `auditFlow` resolves all flow paths relative to `root`; therefore `-root <other-tree>` with the default report name silently writes `docs/runbooks/phase-44-trace-coverage-audit.md` beside the auditor process instead of beside the audited tree (and can overwrite/create the wrong report). The mismatch is between the audited `root` in `run`/`auditFlow` and the report destination in `writeReport`; a relative `-report` contract should have one consistent base.
- **chunk-0025.md:6** — `src/cmd/trace-coverage-audit/main.go:286-297` and `src/cmd/trace-coverage-audit/main_test.go:73-79`: `spanRE` treats `otel.GetTextMapPropagator()` as an instrumented span start and its unit test explicitly locks in that behavior. Propagator registration is tracing configuration, not creation of a span, so a flow containing only that call can be counted toward both its distinct-file threshold and required-file check even though no span is emitted. The canonical risk is false-positive `OK`: propagator setup at `src/cmd/trace-coverage-audit/main.go:286` inflates `MatchedFiles` independently of actual span-start calls such as `tracer.Start`.
- **chunk-0026.md:5** — `src/cmd/unit-drift-validator/main.go:154-155` and `src/cmd/unit-drift-validator/main.go:166-171`: Flag parsing accepts negative `--vehicle`, `--lookback`, and `--cron-interval` values and non-positive lookback/cron values, and the negative vehicle ID is passed to the `OnlyVehicle` path. `OnlyVehicle` bypasses the vehicle repository in `src/internal/worker/unit_drift_validator.go:337-342`, so `--vehicle -1` or another invalid negative ID becomes an `signal_log`/unit-history query for that ID instead of a clear usage error; non-positive windows rely on worker fallbacks rather than rejecting operator input. Cross-file edge: CLI validation should prevent invalid IDs and durations before `worker.Options` is constructed; the worker's zero-value fallback cannot distinguish an omitted option from an explicitly negative CLI value.
- **chunk-0026.md:6** — `src/internal/worker/unit_drift_validator.go:221-232`, `src/internal/worker/unit_drift_validator.go:296-303`, and `src/internal/worker/unit_drift_validator.go:549-552`: Dry-run is stored as mutable global validator state and overwritten by every `Run`/`Start` call. Concurrent invocations with different `DryRun` options can therefore suppress a normal counter increment or emit counters during another call's dry-run pass; the RWMutex protects reads/writes but not which options belong to each invocation. Same-copy state race between concurrent passes: `applyDryRun(opts.DryRun)` is the shared-switch copy, while `incrementDrift`/`incrementCanary` later read the switch, so the call's own dry-run decision is not preserved across the pass.
- **chunk-0026.md:7** — `src/cmd/unit-drift-validator/main.go:163-171` and `src/cmd/resubscribe/main.go:496-508`: The unit-drift CLI claims to mirror `cmd/resubscribe`'s operator identity helper, but its fallback is literal `"unknown"` while resubscribe returns `"unknown@<GOOS>/<GOARCH>"`. An audit log from the new CLI therefore uses an incompatible identity contract when `USER` and `USERNAME` are both absent. Cross-file contract copy differs in the fallback edge: `deriveOperator` in `unit-drift-validator` omits the OS/architecture suffix that the established resubscribe audit helper supplies.
- **chunk-0030.md:5** — `src/internal/adapter/tesla/mapper.go:51-68`, `src/internal/port/external/tesla.go:9-27`, `src/internal/app/vehiclesvc/service.go:91-101`: The legacy Fleet API mapper exposes vendor-state measurements as `BatteryRange`, `OdometerMiles`, `Speed`, and `ChargeRate` without canonical conversion, then the vehicle service persists the range and odometer using legacy mile-named fields. The mapper does not inspect the distance preference or convert odometer/range/speed through the established SI conversion layer. `src/internal/tesla/units/conversions.go` defines canonical distance/speed conversions (miles to meters and mph to m/s), fixed-mile Telemetry fields, and unit-aware behavior; this adapter instead stores raw API numbers with legacy names, risking unit corruption and inconsistent SI semantics when the vehicle preference is kilometers or km/h.
- **chunk-0031.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/eval/runner.go:105-108`, `src/internal/ai/dispatch/dispatch.go:303-316`: `RunGolden` places `g.Input.UserMessage` in `StrategyInput.History` and also sets it as `LastMessage`; the dispatcher appends history and then appends `LastMessage`, so every fast/full/judged golden sends the same user message twice in one request. `GenericStrategy`'s comment explicitly describes the canonical `[system, user]` preamble, but the runner violates it. Other strategies receive duplicate conversational turns in evals, which can change tool choice and answer assertions versus production, where the runner's input typically supplies history without also repeating the latest message as both fields.
- **chunk-0031.md:6** — `src/internal/ai/eval/types.go:38-43`, `src/internal/ai/eval/runner.go:100-108`, `src/internal/ai/strategy/strategy.go:66-77`: `context_overrides` is documented as free-form metadata for a golden's `Strategy.Context`, but `RunGolden` never carries `GoldenInput.ContextOverrides` into `StrategyInput`; `StrategyInput` also has no corresponding field. The runner therefore cannot supply the per-golden vehicle scope or other metadata that YAML authors are told the harness supports. `src/internal/ai/tools/alert/builder.go:79` and `src/internal/ai/tools/automation/automation.go:128` describe production tool scope being supplied through context overrides, while this eval path silently drops the same mechanism, so eval fixtures may falsely pass with missing or default context.
- **chunk-0031.md:7** — `src/internal/ai/eval/judge.go:53-71`, `src/internal/ai/dispatch/dispatch.go:358-365`: `runJudge` dereferences `resp.Message` after `p.Chat` returns without first checking for a nil response. The dispatcher already treats a nil provider response as an error in its chat fallback; `runJudge` instead turns a provider implementation or mock contract violation into a process panic. Judged evaluation can crash the whole CLI/harness instead of reporting a judge-provider error, and the `fakeJudgeProvider`/other provider implementations have no defensive guard covering this path.
- **chunk-0032.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/eval/types.go:38-42`, `src/internal/ai/eval/runner.go:113-117`, `src/internal/ai/strategy/strategy.go:57-65`: `GoldenInput.ContextOverrides` is documented as metadata a real strategy context method can read, but the runner never forwards it and `StrategyInput` has no corresponding field. YAML can therefore declare `context_overrides` successfully (the loader/validator accept arbitrary maps) while the dispatch path silently discards them; evaluation cases cannot exercise the context contract advertised by the schema. No other eval copy forwards the map; `StrategyInput` only carries `UserID`, `LastMessage`, and `History`, so a golden author gets a successful load but no value in `Strategy.Context`.
- **chunk-0032.md:6** — `src/internal/ai/health/ollama_poll.go:229-237`: The Ollama OOM scan performs only one `resp.Body.Read` into 4096 bytes and then ignores the read count/error semantics. A marker beginning after that one read—or split across an HTTP chunk/body read boundary—is treated as healthy, even though a 200 response may carry an OOM indicator in its JSON body; network bodies are not guaranteed to satisfy a single read completely. The Ollama provider and other bounded body readers in the repository use `io.ReadAll(io.LimitReader(..., 4096))`; this poller differs by relying on one transport read, risking false healthy status and no provider suspension.
- **chunk-0032.md:7** — `src/internal/ai/health/ollama_poll.go:237`: The OOM probe uses the substring `"oom"` without a word/JSON value boundary, so ordinary English text containing that sequence (for example `"room"` or `"zoom"`) is falsely classified as an out-of-memory response. It broadens a 200 response health failure based on incidental text rather than a model-error marker such as `out of memory` or an explicit Ollama `{"error":...}` OOM payload; the failure counter and suspension threshold can then trip incorrectly.
- **chunk-0032.md:8** — `src/internal/ai/eval/judge.go:110-126`: `extractJSONObject` counts every `{`/`}` character as nesting and has no JSON-string state. A valid judge reason containing brace characters, such as `{"score":5,"reason":"matched } in answer"}`, is cut at the brace inside the string and rejected as unparseable; nested JSON itself is handled, but braces inside strings are not. The judge contract explicitly asks for a JSON object with an arbitrary natural-language reason, making brace-bearing reasons plausible; parse failures incorrectly fail the golden or score it as judged failure instead of applying the numeric threshold.
- **chunk-0032.md:9** — `src/internal/ai/eval/runner.go:256-260`: `truncate` slices the raw string at a byte index and appends an ellipsis. For answer text containing multibyte UTF-8 near byte 200, `s[:200]` can split a rune and return invalid UTF-8 in assertion reasons/reports instead of preserving the prefix safely. `src/internal/ai/provider/audit.go:448-464` contains an equivalent error truncator that explicitly converts to runes and caps by rune count, showing the established required Unicode-safe behavior.
- **chunk-0033.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/limit/cost.go:250-261`, `src/internal/ai/limit/cost.go:185`, `src/internal/ai/limit/cost.go:202-222`: The cost-cap cache is not keyed or reset by the UTC calendar day. `refreshLocked` accepts any entry younger than `cacheTTL`, and after release `entry.today` is increased regardless of whether midnight has passed. A call crossing 00:00 UTC can therefore enforce yesterday's spend (or yesterday's optimistic release) against today's cap until the TTL expires and is then refetched. `src/internal/ai/limit/limiter.go:394-402` and `src/internal/ai/limit/limiter.go:409-427` explicitly reset per-day counters and calculate UTC-midnight retry times; the cost-cap cache is the missing parallel day-boundary handling. The risk is both false `cost_cap` rejection immediately after midnight and a short window of stale projected spend.
- **chunk-0033.md:6** — `src/internal/ai/provider/anthropic/anthropic.go:376-429`, `src/internal/ai/provider/anthropic/anthropic_test.go:115-137`: The Anthropic stream relay only emits its terminal chunk when it receives `message_stop`; if the HTTP/SSE body ends cleanly after `message_delta` or other frames without that terminal frame, it silently closes the channel. It also does not emit an upstream error when `scanner.Err()` occurs, so dispatch can observe an unexplained stream closure and the audit decorator cannot distinguish it from a normal completion. OpenAI and Azure stream relays handle EOF explicitly (`src/internal/ai/provider/openai/openai.go:367-452`, `src/internal/ai/provider/azure/azure.go:518-625`); OpenAI emits a terminal or error after EOF according to finish reason, and Azure emits its terminal state or an error. Anthropic's parser instead has no EOF terminal/error path, risking premature `Done` semantics and missing audit error attribution.
- **chunk-0034.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/provider/mock/mock.go:168-190`, `src/internal/ai/provider/mock/mock_test.go:94-112`:168: `Reply` exposes `Embedding` and the mock API comments say `Embed` returns the matched reply's vectors, but `Embed` ignores both the looked-up `Reply` and `Reply.Embedding` and always synthesizes hash-derived vectors. Canned/eval callers therefore cannot script embedding behavior, while the implementation silently substitutes unrelated deterministic data.
- **chunk-0034.md:6** — `src/internal/ai/provider/azure/azure.go:249-288`, `src/internal/ai/provider/azure/responses_test.go:166-250`:249: `Embed` allocates `Vectors` with `len(wire.Data)` rather than `len(req.Input)`, so a provider response with missing data leaves the result length shorter than the request and the batch contract is violated. There is no validation that every requested input received one vector, and existing embedding tests do not cover sparse/missing upstream data (the mock tests likewise only cover deterministic complete batches).
- **chunk-0035.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/provider/mock/mock.go:164-179`: `Mock.Embed` ignores the `Reply.Embedding` payload even though `Reply` documents it as producing vectors and `SetReplyByPrompt`/`SetReplyByHash` can select scripted replies. It always derives an 8-dimensional vector from `sha256(model + input)` instead of returning the selected reply. Eval/test callers therefore cannot script deterministic embedding values with the mock API. `src/internal/ai/provider/mock/mock_test.go:93-111` only tests default determinism and does not cover `Reply.Embedding`, so the implemented behavior diverges from the public mock contract without detection.
- **chunk-0035.md:6** — `src/internal/ai/rag/chunker.go:112-164`: `splitLargeParagraph` splits sentence text and drops the separator punctuation instead of preserving it. Every call to `splitOnAny` removes `.`, `!`, and `?` occurrences together with their following delimiter, then `splitLargeParagraph` joins fragments with spaces; oversized prose is rewritten before chunk embedding/indexing. The same implementation also applies nested splits through slices whose backing storage is reused, which can overwrite still-unvisited fragments when one separator expands to several output fragments. `src/internal/ai/rag/docs_indexer.go:89`, `src/internal/ai/rag/lexical_docs.go:63`, and other chunk consumers receive corrupted/non-semantic text and may embed or index different content than the source document.
- **chunk-0035.md:7** — `src/internal/ai/rag/chunker.go:172-189`: `hardSplit` can emit invalid UTF-8 despite its rune-boundary contract. If `maxBytes < utf8.RuneLen` for a single multibyte rune, the function walks `cut` back to zero and has no valid UTF-8 boundary to fall back to, but then slices at `cut == maxBytes` and corrupts the chunk. The function comment claims this pathological case is unreachable, but callers accept an arbitrary positive `maxBytes` and tests explicitly exercise small limits on multibyte input. `src/internal/ai/rag/chunker_test.go:73-96` only passes `maxBytes=50`, which is larger than the test rune width, so it misses the corruption boundary; valid UTF-8 production/RAG invariants break for limits such as 1 or 2 bytes.
- **chunk-0036.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/rag/chunker.go:161-179`, `src/internal/ai/rag/chunker_test.go:64-78`: `hardSplit` can emit invalid UTF-8 when `maxBytes` is positive but smaller than the byte width of a leading multibyte rune. If `maxBytes=1` and the text starts with `é`, the backward rune-boundary scan reaches `cut == 0`; the fallback sets `cut=maxBytes` and slices the first byte of the rune, producing malformed UTF-8 despite the function's byte/rune-boundary contract. `TestChunkText_HardSplitOnRuneBoundary` covers only `maxBytes=50` with multibyte text, so it misses the one-byte limit edge case. The public contract accepts any positive limit through `ChunkText`, and callers would receive chunks that cannot round-trip as valid UTF-8.
- **chunk-0038.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/strategies/alert-tuning-suggestions/strategy.go:65-76`, `src/internal/ai/strategies/alert-tuning-suggestions/goldens.yaml:7-12`, `src/internal/ai/strategies/alert-tuning-suggestions/canned/typical_noisy_battery_threshold.yaml:1-24`: The alert-tuning prompt, golden, and canned reply describe changing `battery_level` from a 20 threshold to 15 as “raise” the threshold. The rule uses `<`, so the numeric threshold is being lowered from 20 to 15 and the tool test comments correctly call this a tighter/lower-noise threshold. The same example appears in the production prompt and the eval replay/canned narrative, so the model can repeat the wrong numeric-direction wording even while proposing the correct `15` value. Owners reviewing the patch may misunderstand the direction of change; the prompt, golden, canned reply, and rationale should consistently say “lower/tighten”.
- **chunk-0038.md:6** — `src/internal/ai/strategies/alert-pack-builder/goldens.yaml:38-51`, `src/internal/ai/strategies/alert-pack-builder/canned/comprehensive_pack_request.yaml:1-13`, `src/internal/ai/strategies/alert-pack-builder/strategy.go:12-31`, `src/internal/alertpacks/catalog.go:161-169`, `src/internal/alertpacks/expanded.go:1-108`: The comprehensive golden requires every applicable template in the shared custom catalog, and the strategy/context explicitly expose that catalog, but the canned comprehensive response sends only nine template IDs while claiming “every supported event in one place.” `CustomCatalog()` is built from deduplicated base plus expanded templates, so the canned replay omits the majority of supported catalog IDs but still calls itself a Full Catalog Sweep. A judge or offline eval can therefore pass an answer that is materially inconsistent with the catalog-completeness rubric rather than proving comprehensive coverage.
- **chunk-0040.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/tools/lifetime/stats_qa.go:250-282`, `src/internal/ai/tools/lifetime/stats_qa.go:357-365`, `src/internal/api/ailifetime/handler.go:264-301`, `src/internal/api/lifetime/handler.go:121-129`, `src/internal/api/lifetime/handler.go:180-195`, `src/internal/api/lifetime/handler.go:385-392`: The lifetime-stats AI and canonical lifetime handlers expose legacy display-unit contracts (`total_distance_km`, `longest_drive_km`, `highest_speed_kmh`, `total_energy_kwh`, `avg_efficiency_wh_km`) instead of SI-canonical API fields, despite the canonical SQL/source comments explicitly saying distance/duration/speed are stored canonically in meters, seconds, and m/s. The migration rule requires SI fields and display conversion only at the React boundary; these tool names lock converted km/km-h/kWh values into the AI envelope. The canonical computation currently converts SI database aggregates into km/min/kWh/km-h in `src/internal/api/lifetime/handler.go`, and `src/internal/api/ailifetime/handler.go` reshapes those values into `LifetimeStatsEnvelope` unchanged. A second copy is therefore not an isolated tool adapter: downstream AI consumers and tests can be written against legacy units, risking double display conversion or incorrect answers when unit preferences change.
- **chunk-0040.md:6** — `src/internal/ml/chargingcurves/clustering.go:193-202`, `src/internal/ml/chargingcurves/clustering.go:372-376`, `src/internal/ml/chargingcurves/clustering.go:397-406`, `src/internal/ai/tools/charge/curve_clusters.go:278-287`: The ML charging-cluster envelope derives duration through the legacy `DurationMinutes()` helper and emits `duration_min_mean`, while the trainer and tool already otherwise use SI fields (`peak_power_w_*`, `avg_power_w_*`, `total_energy_wh_*`). This mixes minutes into an otherwise SI-canonical charging payload and names a new Go/JSON field with the prohibited `_min` legacy suffix. `src/internal/models/charging/charging.go:55-60` implements the legacy minutes conversion, while `ChargingSession.StartedAt`/`EndedAt` can provide the canonical duration in seconds. The current output has no canonical seconds field and can diverge from the Phase-48 rule for charging-session duration.
- **chunk-0042.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/tools/digest/digest.go:89-91`, `src/internal/ai/tools/digest/digest.go:158-175`, `src/internal/ai/strategies/digest-narration/canned/standard_week.yaml:9-12`, `src/internal/ai/strategies/digest-narration/goldens.yaml:27-40`: The weekly-digest tool contract and aggregation return canonical SI keys (`drives_distance_m`, `charges_energy_added_wh`), but the canned answer and golden expectation present and require legacy display units (`142 miles`, `38 kWh`, and a negative `miles`/`kWh charged` check). `src/internal/ai/tools/coaching/coaching.go:98-105` similarly declares an SI envelope and returns distance/energy in meters/watt-hours, while `src/internal/ai/strategies/drive-coaching/canned/efficient_drive.yaml:18-21` and `aggressive_drive_with_hard_braking.yaml:18-21` hardcode km, km/h, and kWh narration. The canned output bypasses a user-unit display conversion and can teach/test presentations that are inconsistent with the SI tool contract, risking canonical-unit drift and the wrong unit shown to a user.
- **chunk-0046.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/stream/writer.go:206-228`, `src/internal/ai/stream/writer.go:482-492`, `src/internal/ai/stream/writer_test.go:96-110`: `Writer.Send` is documented and declared safe for concurrent use, but concurrent callers plus `shutdown` can send on a channel that `shutdown` closes. `Send` selects on `w.ch <- frame` while `shutdown` calls `close(w.ch)` and does not synchronize producers; a concurrent `Close`/`WriteDone`/`WriteError`/stall teardown can therefore win the race and panics can occur from sending on the closed channel. This is an edge case distinct from ordinary closed-write detection: `closed.Load()` and `closedCh` are checked before the send, but the channel close is asynchronous; the test coverage only exercises sequential sends/closes and consumer failures, not a close racing with a blocked concurrent producer.
- **chunk-0050.md:4** — `src/internal/ai/tools/maintenance/predictive.go:276-318`, `src/internal/ai/tools/maintenance/predictive.go:390-420`, `src/internal/ai/tools/maintenance/predictive.go:465-472`, `src/internal/ai/tools/maintenance/predictive_test.go:278-300`: The predictive-maintenance tool exposes legacy mileage contracts (`current_mileage`, `due_mileage`, `last_service_mileage`, `interval_miles`, `mileage`) for odometer/service data instead of defining a canonical SI-distance contract in meters. It explicitly maps its envelope to the legacy operator baseline field names, and its description/test contract teaches downstream narration and tool consumers to interpret those values as miles. This is the same canonical-unit drift seen in neighboring AI lifetime/digest tool findings: values can be narrated in legacy units or mixed with SI-based calculations, and a user-facing conversion may be applied twice or omitted. Distance fields in this AI envelope should use canonical meters or an explicit display-boundary conversion contract, with unknown/null semantics preserved by pointers rather than sentinel numbers.
- **chunk-0053.md:5** — `src/internal/ai/tools/forecast/period_compare.go:180-198`, `src/internal/ai/tools/forecast/period_compare.go:251-268`, `src/internal/ai/tools/forecast/period_compare_test.go:180-190`, `src/internal/api/periodstats/handler.go:68-76`: `days_a` and `days_b` use a plain `int` plus a zero sentinel but both omitted and explicit `0` are represented by the same value, while the tool also uses zero as the “default 30/90 days” sentinel in `Execute`. This contradicts the tool contract: it validates explicit zero as “all time,” yet immediately replaces zero with 30 or 90 before calling the canonical comparator. The canonical period-stats helper explicitly treats `days <= 0` as all-time (and the baseline comments/tests preserve the explicit-zero contract). The adjacent test only asserts that validation accepts zero and separately tests defaulting for an omitted payload, so it misses that an explicit zero is converted into a default window; narration can silently omit older data. The input needs pointer/optional presence semantics (or an equivalent sentinel), with Execute preserving explicit zero and defaulting only an omitted field.
- **chunk-0059.md:5** — `src/internal/api/aibatthealth/handler.go:111-129`, `src/internal/api/aichargcurve/handler.go:76-94`, `src/internal/api/aiclimate/handler.go:152-187`, `src/internal/api/aicostfcst/handler.go:121-145`: The four strict JSON-body parsers call only a single `json.Decoder.Decode`; they reject malformed first objects and unknown fields, but do not check for a second JSON value. Input such as `{"vehicle_id":42} {"vehicle_id":43}` is accepted as valid after decoding the first object, so malformed/trailing data can reach the SSE or provider path. The same decoder shape is copied across four AI handlers in this chunk; an HTTP JSON body contract should require EOF after one object. Add the same trailing-value check to all four parsers (or a strict shared decoder).
- **chunk-0060.md:5** — `src/internal/api/aidatarep/handler.go:136-143`, `src/internal/api/aifeedtri/handler.go:146-158`: Both small-body handlers cap input with `io.LimitReader` but do not read one extra byte or reject a body larger than the advertised limit. Each then decodes the truncated prefix and treats it as accepted JSON, so whitespace padding or any data after exactly 16 KiB can be silently ignored; `aidatarep` in particular documents a 16-KiB cap but has no exact-size guard. Other bounded AI handlers use the canonical `LimitReader(...+1)` plus explicit size check and/or return HTTP 413 (for example `aipostcard/handler.go:114-123`, `aisuggeo/handler.go:116-123`); missing the extra-byte check creates inconsistent rejection behavior and permits oversized request bodies through the AI route.
- **chunk-0060.md:6** — `src/internal/api/aidigest/handler.go:62-82`, `src/internal/api/aidrivesearch/handler.go:91-104`: These handlers decode request JSON directly from `r.Body` with no byte cap and do not reject unknown fields. Large bodies can reach the JSON decoder before the handler's logical prompt or week bounds are checked, and typo/extra-field bodies can be accepted instead of being rejected at the transport boundary. The adjacent bounded handlers in this snapshot (`aicostfcst/handler.go:129-142`, `aicrossrule/handler.go:136-151`) use strict decoding, while several AI APIs use explicit 16-KiB limits; this inconsistency can allow unbounded allocations and silently ignore unrecognized request fields before AI provider work.
- **chunk-0061.md:5** — `src/internal/api/ailifetime/handler.go:107-126`, `src/internal/api/aiinboxcat/handler.go:199-210`, `src/internal/api/aimlanom/handler.go:121-130`: Three AI POST parsers read without an advertised body-size cap: lifetime Q&A streams directly to `json.Decoder`, inbox categorization streams directly to a strict decoder, and learned anomaly decoding streams directly to a single decoder. A missing/oversized request can consume memory and reach provider-dispatch setup work; the anomaly parser also decodes only one unknown field set and does not call `DisallowUnknownFields`, while its sibling strict AI parsers reject unknown JSON. The bounded AI parsers in `aigeofautom/handler.go:158-177`, `aifeedtri/handler.go:146-158`, and `ailogtrace/handler.go:158-170` establish the expected small request envelope; without matching limits these three handlers have inconsistent transport and DoS behavior.
- **chunk-0061.md:6** — `src/internal/api/aifeedtri/handler.go:146-158`, `src/internal/api/aifsmnar/handler.go:141-152`, `src/internal/api/ailogtrace/handler.go:158-169`, `src/internal/api/aiincident/handler.go:122-135`: These body parsers advertise a 16-KiB ceiling but use `io.LimitReader(..., 16*1024)` without reading one extra byte, so a body of 16,385+ bytes is truncated to 16 KiB and may then decode successfully if the valid JSON prefix is complete, silently treating whitespace or additional trailing JSON as within the limit. Canonical guarded handlers use `LimitReader(limit+1)` and explicitly return 413 for the oversize case, including `aigeofautom/handler.go:160-164` and patterns already noted in the prior chunks; missing the exact-size guard permits oversized bodies inconsistently across AI routes.
- **chunk-0061.md:7** — `src/internal/api/ailifetime/handler.go:117-126`, `src/internal/api/aimlanom/handler.go:121-130`, `src/internal/api/aigeofautom/handler.go:175-177`, `src/internal/api/aifsmnar/handler.go:150-153`, `src/internal/api/aiinboxcat/handler.go:204-210`, `src/internal/api/ailogtrace/handler.go:167-170`, `src/internal/api/aifeedtri/handler.go:155-158`, `src/internal/api/aiincident/handler.go:131-134`: Most parsers decode only the first JSON value and do not verify EOF or that the body contains exactly one JSON object. A payload such as `{"vehicle_id":42,"question":"q"}{"extra":true}` or valid-object-plus-garbage can decode successfully, depending on the parser, because no second decode/EOF check is performed; strict unknown-field checks do not cover a second top-level value. Adjacent API parsers are expected to reject malformed/trailing transport data at the HTTP boundary before provider or tool work. `json.Unmarshal` in `aiincident` also accepts any trailing valid JSON document only as far as the map decoder currently intended, but the incident comment says no fields are meaningful; this still permits malformed envelope ambiguity and is inconsistent with strict sibling handlers.
- **chunk-0061.md:8** — `src/internal/api/ailifetime/handler.go:117-126`, `src/internal/api/ailifetime/handler_test.go:143-166`, `src/internal/api/aimlanom/handler_test.go:192-204`: Lifetime Q&A checks character bounds with `len(req.Question)` (bytes), so a 1024-rune question containing non-ASCII text can be rejected below the documented rune cap; conversely whitespace-only and padded text pass because only an exact-empty-string check is used. Separately, the learned-anomaly test implements a private copy of production validation and even a second copy in production would not be exercised; the test does not call `(*Handler).ServeHTTP` and can pass while production decoding or validation drifts. The UI contract calls the lifetime limit a character cap (`aiLifetimeStatsQAMaxQuestionChars`) but UTF-8 byte length changes acceptance. The anomaly test copy explicitly mirrors validation, so it is a false-positive scaffold rather than a load-bearing production test (the source comments acknowledge this is stripped code).
- **chunk-0062.md:5** — `src/internal/api/aimlanom/handler.go:121-130`, `src/internal/api/aimlchargcv/handler.go:121-132`, `src/internal/api/aimlrange/handler.go:104-116`: The three learned-ML handlers decode request bodies directly from `r.Body` with a single `json.Decoder.Decode` call and do not enforce a size limit, reject unknown fields, or require EOF after the first object. Oversized bodies can reach provider/tool setup, concatenated JSON values can be accepted as the first object, and field typos silently use zero/default values in the learned anomaly/range/charging trainers. The neighboring AI parsers in this snapshot establish bounded, strict request envelopes; inconsistency here permits transport/data-envelope ambiguity before provider work.
- **chunk-0062.md:6** — `src/internal/api/aimqttsse/handler.go:233-250`, `src/internal/api/ainldash/handler.go:172-189`, `src/internal/api/ainlgrafana/handler.go:214-231`: These strict small-body parsers advertise a 16-KiB cap but read with an exact-size `io.LimitReader`, then decode only the first JSON value. Bodies larger than the cap may be truncated at 16 KiB and still decode successfully, while valid-object-plus-trailing-data is accepted because there is no second decode/EOF check. Canonical guarded parsers in adjacent AI routes read `limit+1` and reject oversized input explicitly, then also require one complete JSON document. These three otherwise-strict handlers retain the size/EOF inconsistency under different prompts and tool scopes.
- **chunk-0063.md:5** — `src/internal/api/ainlsql/handler.go:178-192`, `src/internal/api/aipiiredact/handler.go:127-140`, `src/internal/api/aipredmaint/handler.go:137-150`, `src/internal/api/aiquiethrs/handler.go:185-198`: These four small-body parsers read through an exact-size `io.LimitReader` and decode only the first JSON value. An exact-cap or larger body is silently truncated before JSON validation, while concatenated objects after the first value are accepted; malformed or oversized input can therefore reach provider/tool paths instead of receiving a consistent 400/413 rejection. Several neighboring AI handlers use `LimitReader(...+1)` plus an explicit size check (for example `aipostcard/handler.go:114-122`), and strict JSON decode should also require EOF. The four copies differ only in request field names and range validation, so preserving distinct contracts does not justify accepting different EOF/limit behavior.
- **chunk-0063.md:6** — `src/internal/api/aiperiodcmp/handler.go:92-97`: The period-compare parser decodes directly from the request body with no size cap and performs no EOF/trailing-value check. An unbounded body can be read by the decoder, and `{"vehicle_id":42} {"vehicle_id":43}` is accepted from the first object before any period bounds or provider work. Other handlers in this chunk cap small request bodies at 16 KiB. Here the in-scope `vehicle_id`/days envelope has no equivalent transport bound, and manual unknown-key rejection does not fix a second top-level JSON value.
- **chunk-0063.md:7** — `src/internal/api/airaghelp/handler.go:163-177`: The RAG help handler decodes `r.Body` directly with no size or byte limit, no `DisallowUnknownFields`, and no check for trailing JSON values. A giant body or concatenated JSON object can bypass the advertised prompt character cap and transport validation before SSE/provider setup. `maxPromptChars` checks the decoded string after unbounded decoding, not the HTTP body. It also does not reject unknown keys; `airaghelp/handler_test.go` has a mirrored pre-stream validator that does not establish strict production decoding either.
- **chunk-0063.md:8** — `src/internal/api/aipostcard/handler.go:128-139`: The postcard parser uses `json.Unmarshal`, which ignores unknown fields. A valid `trip_id` plus a misspelled field or other unrelated key is accepted and can drive provider/tool prompting even though the wire schema is limited to `trip_id` and `style_hint`; this is especially risky if the in-scope envelope is later extended or typoed. The neighboring strict handlers use `Decoder.DisallowUnknownFields`; this package also has canonical bad-body tests for malformed inputs, but no unknown-field or trailing-field contract. The size guard itself correctly uses `LimitReader(maxBodyBytes+1)` and is not part of this bug.
- **chunk-0064.md:5** — `src/internal/api/airaghelp/handler.go:166-170`, `src/internal/api/aisearch/handler.go:96-100`, `src/internal/api/aisettingsvalidate/handler.go:138-144`, `src/internal/api/aismartcharge/handler.go:125-131`: These four request parsers accept bodies without an explicit size bound, strict unknown-field policy, or EOF/trailing-value check. The first three decode directly from `r.Body`; smart-charge adds `DisallowUnknownFields` but still accepts concatenated JSON values and has no body cap. An oversized or trailing-value request can reach provider/tool or validation work, inconsistent with the bounded, strict AI transport parsers in the snapshot. The adjacent AI handlers repeatedly use strict decoding plus explicit limits; for example `src/internal/api/aisafetyexp/handler.go:153-163` and `src/internal/api/aisignalnl/handler.go:172-183` read a bounded buffer, while `src/internal/api/aicostfcst/handler.go:129-142` and `src/internal/api/aipostcard/handler.go:114-123` enforce strict EOF-capable parsing. Missing the same transport contract permits unbounded reads and ambiguous two-object JSON bodies.
- **chunk-0064.md:6** — `src/internal/api/aisafetyexp/handler.go:153-163`, `src/internal/api/aisignalnl/handler.go:172-183`: Both handlers advertise 16-KiB body caps via `io.LimitReader`, but read exactly the cap rather than `limit+1` and do not report HTTP 413 or otherwise reject oversized bodies. Input larger than the cap is silently truncated to a JSON prefix; if the first JSON object ends exactly at the cap (or before ignored trailing whitespace/data), it can be decoded and accepted while the excess is never inspected. Canonical bounded readers in the adjacent AI packages use `LimitReader(..., maxBodyBytes+1)` and an explicit length check; truncation without an extra-byte check also differs across AI routes and can bypass declared body-size limits.
- **chunk-0064.md:7** — `src/internal/api/airaghelp/handler.go:168-179`, `src/internal/api/aisearch/handler.go:98-109`: These two prompt handlers count the validated prompt with `len(prompt)` and therefore enforce a byte limit, while the constants are documented and surfaced as character limits (`4096 characters`). A multibyte UTF-8 prompt can be rejected hundreds of characters earlier than intended, while strings containing fewer runes than the cap but more bytes are handled inconsistently. This same pattern is duplicated across two packages; use a rune/character count (for example `utf8.RuneCountInString(strings.TrimSpace(...))`) so the HTTP boundary matches the documented prompt contract.
- **chunk-0064.md:8** — `src/internal/api/aismartcharge/handler.go:45`, `src/internal/api/airouteeff/handler.go:198`: Two AI prompt/response surfaces introduce or echo legacy display-unit wording/field names: the smart-charge request defines `battery_capacity_kwh`, while route-efficiency explicitly asks the model for a `"kWh/100mi"` figure. The active SI canonical contract requires new API data to use SI (`Wh`/meters), with display conversion only at the UI boundary; retaining legacy wire text creates an avoidable unit-contract exception and unit drift risk. These names are inconsistent with SI canonical fields elsewhere and can leak legacy values through provider prompts or API clients. The route-efficiency handler should make the prompt metric unit explicit in canonical terms or consume canonical metadata rather than hardcoding kWh/100mi.
- **chunk-0065.md:5** — `src/internal/api/aismartcharge/handler.go:125-131`, `src/internal/api/aisuggeo/handler.go:131-138`, `src/internal/api/aiswupd/handler.go:131-140`, `src/internal/api/aitconar/handler.go:109-115`, `src/internal/api/aitempimpact/handler.go:116-122`, `src/internal/api/aitirepress/handler.go:165-171`, `src/internal/api/aitripplanllm/handler.go:125-131`: The seven strict JSON parsers in this chunk decode only the first JSON value. A second object/value after the first valid object is ignored; for example `{"vehicle_id":42} {"vehicle_id":43}` passes validation and reaches provider/dispatcher/SSE paths. Five parsers decode directly from `r.Body` without a transport size bound; the bounded readers in `aisuggeo` and `aiswupd` have separate boundary/size-defect risks below. The same single-Decode shape appears across all seven files in this chunk; the chunk should require EOF after one object and should use a bounded-read-plus-extra-byte pattern consistently. Other AI handlers use explicit bounded reads and EOF/trailing checks, so this route family will drift on malformed-body and oversized-body behavior.
- **chunk-0065.md:6** — `src/internal/api/aiswupd/handler.go:122-124`: The software-update summarizer caps reads at exactly 16 KiB (`io.LimitReader(..., 16*1024)`) but does not read `MaxBodyBytes+1` or explicitly reject a body larger than the advertised cap. A valid JSON prefix plus whitespace or any data beyond the cutoff can be truncated and accepted because decoding still succeeds. `aisuggeo/handler.go:116-123` in this same chunk and several bounded AI handlers use the correct extra-byte read plus explicit 413 rejection; without the same check, `aiswupd` silently accepts payloads outside its 16-KiB contract.
- **chunk-0065.md:7** — `src/internal/api/aitempimpact/handler.go:264-270,311-319`, `src/internal/api/tempimpact/repo.go:50-51,68-69,87-88`: The AI temperature-impact copy repeats the baseline temperature-page SQL formula with a 1609.344 divisor, yet names the result `avg_battery_pct_per_100km` and says the response is battery consumption per 100 km. Dividing SI meters by the meters-per-mile constant produces a distance in miles, so the same values can be exported and narrated as km values while actually representing per-100-miles style mileage. `tempimpact/repo.go` contains the same formula and `driveStatsMetersPerMile` constant, and `aitempimpact` duplicates it in both bucket and trend queries; either both implementations should use one canonical SI 100-km formula or the contracts/labels must be corrected together.
- **chunk-0066.md:5** — `src/internal/api/user/handler.go:64-82`: `Login` accepts every password for the username `"admin"`; it checks only that the username is admin, then mints a 24-hour admin token. This is an authentication bypass rather than a harmless legacy default, and there is no equivalent password-verification dependency or credential source in the handler. The test suite currently codifies `"admin any password"` and `"admin empty password"` as successes (`src/internal/api/user/handler_test.go:258-266`), so the bug is shipped behavior and would only be fixed by changing both implementation and tests; neighboring authentication code (for example `src/internal/api/auth/handler.go`) is OAuth, so there is no canonical app-password checker to reuse here.
- **chunk-0066.md:6** — `src/internal/api/vehicle/handler.go:78-80,252-260`, `src/internal/api/drives/detail.go:204-215,240-247`, `src/internal/api/locsnap/handler.go:35-38`: Three position/history projections label the canonical `VehicleSpeed` signal as `speed_mph`. `VehicleSpeed` is SI in m/s in the telemetry pipeline, but each of these response fields preserves a legacy mph-style name without converting the value. Consumers can therefore display raw m/s as mph; the same field-name contract conflicts with the SI canonical naming mandate. `src/internal/api/vehicle/handler_test.go:137-173` and the drive-detail/loc-snapshot tests preserve the `speed_mph`/`speed` alias instead of asserting SI units, allowing the mismatch to pass. Canonical display conversion belongs at the frontend boundary, so the wire projection should expose a clear SI field (or a documented legacy compatibility field if deliberately preserving it), not an mph suffix carrying an m/s value.
- **chunk-0066.md:7** — `src/internal/api/totp/handler.go:620-638`, `src/internal/api/tripplanner/handler.go:62-66`, `src/internal/api/tripplanner/confidence.go:103-107`, `src/internal/api/user/handler.go:64-70`, `src/internal/api/vehicleaccess/handler.go:208-211`: Five chunk handlers decode request bodies independently: four use a plain `json.Decoder.Decode` with no size limit, strict unknown-field mode, or EOF/trailing-value check, while the TOTP body is capped and strict but still accepts trailing JSON because it performs only one decode. A single-object plus second JSON value, unknown fields, or oversized unbounded payloads can therefore reach handler logic inconsistently or consume memory. Bounded strict decoders elsewhere in the API perform the body-limit/unknown-field plus EOF discipline, and the catalog requires API client/error and validation contracts to use shared canonical behavior; these copy variants are maintenance-prone and give different malformed-body behavior for similar POST endpoints.
- **chunk-0067.md:3** — `src/internal/api/aiyir/handler.go:99-103`, `src/internal/api/aiyir/handler_test.go:203-207`: The year-in-review handler decodes `r.Body` directly with one `json.Decoder.Decode`; it has neither a body-size cap, `DisallowUnknownFields`, nor a second-value/EOF check. Oversized bodies are accepted for allocation/processing, unknown fields can be silently ignored, and concatenated JSON objects can pass validation. The package-local `validateOnly` test helper repeats the same incomplete single decode, so bad-input coverage does not exercise or prevent the missing checks. Adjacent AI handlers in this and previous snapshots already use strict one-object decoders and bounded read helpers; for example `src/internal/api/alerts/alert_rules.go:780-812` and `src/internal/api/alerts/alerts.go:280-299` reject unknown fields, trailing values, and bodies over their caps.
- **chunk-0067.md:4** — `src/internal/api/alertmsg/handler.go:181-192`, `src/internal/api/alertmsg/handler_test.go:527-536`: `MessagePreview` caps the request by reading `maxAlertRequestBodyBytes` through `LimitReader`, but unlike `maxAlertRequestBodyBytes+1` readers in the neighboring alerts package it performs no extra-byte check. A payload larger than 1 MiB whose first 1 MiB bytes already contain a complete valid JSON object is parsed successfully, and the test suite explicitly documents/reduces oversized-prefix behavior rather than asserting rejection. `src/internal/api/alerts/alert_rules.go:780-788` and `src/internal/api/alerts/alerts.go:280-287` read limit-plus-one and reject `len(body) > limit`, so alert-message previews can accept a body rejected by the same-domain alert-rule endpoints.
- **chunk-0067.md:5** — `src/internal/api/alertmsg/handler.go:189-192`, `src/internal/api/alertmsg/handler_test.go:420-489`: `MessagePreview` uses plain `json.Unmarshal`, so misspelled or unsupported request fields are silently ignored instead of being rejected at the transport boundary. The same preview request family has a strict counterpart in `src/internal/api/alerts/alert_rules.go:780-812`, which both fingerprints fields and rejects unknown fields; a typo such as `signalName` or `valueNum` can therefore fall through to defaults and produce a successful preview for a payload the UI/API contract does not accept. The neighboring strict request decoder in the alerts package rejects unknown DTO fields and trailing JSON, while this preview accepts any keys not in `alertMessagePreviewRequest`; preview behavior diverges from create/update/test alert request validation.
- **chunk-0067.md:6** — `src/internal/api/alerts/alerts.go:306-322`, `src/internal/api/alerts/alerts_test.go:369-390`: `normalizeNote` validates the length with `len(trimmed)`, which counts UTF-8 bytes, but its API contract/error text says the limit is characters. Multibyte text can therefore be rejected below 1000 runes (for example excessive-byte-content repeated in an accent-heavy string), while different Unicode encodings of the same visible character length produce different acceptance outcomes. The same package uses a rune-count policy for message templates (`src/internal/api/alerts/alert_rules.go:842-858`), so alert notes and alert templates apply inconsistent character-length rules.
- **chunk-0068.md:5** — `src/internal/api/analytics/queries.go:188-238`, `src/internal/api/analytics/queries.go:635-650`: Charging-session totals use `len(sessions)` rather than the number of sessions accepted by the endpoint's `cutoff`/`endCutoff` window. The session loop correctly skips rows outside the requested start/end bounds, but `fleetSessions += len(sessions)` increments the total with every returned row, including out-of-range rows; a bounded query also omits sessions if the repository returns more than the cap or if window filtering changes the set. Drive totals in the adjacent loop use a filtered `driveCount`, so the charging metric is inconsistent and can report sessions/energy/count mismatches for the same range. The repository call is shared per vehicle, but each vehicle's `sessions` slice is reduced by date checks in this handler. This copy should maintain a `sessionCount` accumulator alongside the charging totals, increment only for sessions inside the window, rather than trusting slice length.
- **chunk-0068.md:6** — `src/internal/api/api_call_log_middleware.go:318-350`: Truncated JSON request/response bodies are not safely redacted. `redactBodyBytes` treats any over-limit JSON as JSON, but `redactJSONBody` attempts to decode the capped prefix; if the body was cut mid-JSON object, decoding fails and the function returns the original bytes unchanged. The middleware then appends the truncation marker and stores those raw bytes, so secret or PII fields occurring inside the first 10 KB can bypass recursive redaction whenever the body is truncated. Both request and response capture paths call `redactBodyBytes`; the same `redactJSONBody` implementation exposes both audit body fields. Capping alone prevents storage volume but must also preserve redaction or use a fail-safe opaque/redacted representation when partial JSON cannot be parsed.
- **chunk-0068.md:7** — `src/internal/api/anomaly/handler.go:327-335`: The 24-hour anomaly count accepts timestamps in the future: it checks only `now.Sub(t) < 24*time.Hour`, so a timestamp parsed successfully but later than `now` produces a negative duration and is counted even though it is not within the preceding 24-hour window. Future-dated trend or database evidence can therefore inflate `anomalies_last_24h` and the HTTP/AI snapshot. The same anomaly list also has unrelated timestamp semantics elsewhere (`since := time.Now().AddDate...`, trend timestamps from `time.Now()`), but `anomalies_last_24h` needs an explicit half-open interval (`t <= now` and `t >= now-24h`) rather than a one-sided upper duration check.
- **chunk-0069.md:5** — `src/internal/api/apibulk/helpers.go:67-95`: Both shared bulk decoders perform only a first-value `Decode` after enabling `DisallowUnknownFields`; they do not check that EOF follows the first object. Payloads such as `{"ids":[1]} {"ids":[2]}` therefore pass validation and execute the first operation while silently ignoring trailing JSON. This is the same malformed-transport class found across strict handlers in neighboring chunks; neither bulk callers nor tests in `src/internal/api/apibulk/helpers_test.go` validate one-object-only bodies, so invalid data can affect real deletes/updates.
- **chunk-0069.md:6** — `src/internal/api/apiflagsh/handler.go:125-132`: The feature-flag `PUT` body uses an unbounded, single-value decoder with no `DisallowUnknownFields` and no EOF/trailing-value check. Unknown field typos and a second JSON value are accepted; an unbounded body may also allocate before the flag store is updated. Adjacent bulk and flag-administration helpers use strict decoder patterns, and `src/internal/api/apikey/handler.go:154-157` has the same weak single-decode shape.
- **chunk-0069.md:7** — `src/internal/api/apikey/handler.go:154-157`: API-key creation decodes directly into the request struct without unknown-field rejection, trailing-value validation, or a byte limit. A second object or a large extra payload can be accepted after the first valid object, and `app_token`/permission validation only sees the first decoded value. The same weak one-value transport pattern appears in `src/internal/api/apiflagsh/handler.go:125-132`; strict body parsers such as `src/internal/api/apibulk/helpers.go:67-95` at least reject unknown fields, though they still lack the EOF check.
- **chunk-0069.md:8** — `src/internal/api/apikey_middleware.go:23-77`, `src/internal/api/apikey_middleware.go:180-196`: `APIKeyAuth` and `APIKeyAuthRequired` pass an arbitrary `*database.DB` directly to `findAPIKeyByHash`; if the database object exists but `Pool` is nil, `findAPIKeyByHash` dereferences `db.Pool` and panics instead of failing authentication. This differs from the nearby `AppTokenAuth` path at `src/internal/api/apikey_middleware.go:104-155`, which explicitly injects a nullable `database.DBTX` and returns 503 when no database/connection is available.
- **chunk-0070.md:5** — `src/internal/api/auth/handler.go:66-78`, `src/internal/api/auth/handler.go:80-118`: The OAuth login flow generates and returns an opaque state but the callback never receives or validates that state: it only reads `code`. A login attacker can therefore complete a callback without proving correlation with the prior login, creating an OAuth authorization-code/CSRF mix-up risk. There is no server-side session or cookie binding the state to the callback request. `src/internal/api/auth/handler_test.go:174-220` verifies that login embeds the generated state in the authorization URL, but no callback test verifies state presence or equality, so the missing protection is untested. A state-consistency check must be implemented and pinned by tests.
- **chunk-0070.md:6** — `src/internal/api/audit/handler.go:137-151`: `decodeRevealAuditBody` performs only one `json.Decoder.Decode` and does not require EOF. Bodies containing a valid first object followed by another value, whitespace, or junk are accepted as the first object rather than rejected as a non-single JSON payload. Such trailing content can be accepted by the masked-reveal audit endpoint and written to `audit_logs`. The stricter canonical pattern is used in `src/internal/api/alerts/alerts.go:277-300`, `src/internal/api/impersonate/handler.go:360-376`, and other handlers in the repository: decode once, then require EOF/`single JSON object` before application validation. This decoder lacks that same trailing-data protection.
- **chunk-0071.md:5** — `src/internal/api/automation/test_run.go:47-65`, `src/internal/api/automation/test_run.go:304-319`: `TestRun` initializes `allMet=true`, but when a condition evaluates to `unknown` it only sets `hasUnknown=true`; it never changes `allMet`. Unknown live-state/location/variable conditions therefore produce `"conditions_met": true`, and `simulateActions` treats their actions as executable rather than unknown/blocked. This can make a dry-run falsely report successful validation even though the result could not be evaluated. The response explicitly has separate `HasUnknownConditions` metadata, so `allMet` is not safe to leave true; unknown must drive an un-met/blocked condition plan while preserving the per-condition `"unknown"` reason.
- **chunk-0071.md:6** — `src/internal/api/automation/handler.go:290-295`, `src/internal/api/automation/handler.go:1253-1258`, `src/internal/api/automation/routines.go:345-350`, `src/internal/api/backup/restore_handler.go:72-76`, `src/internal/api/backup/restore_handler.go:102-106`: Five state-changing/configuration handlers in this chunk decode JSON from `r.Body` with a single permissive `json.Decoder.Decode`; they accept trailing JSON values, unknown fields, and in the automation cases do not enforce a bounded body. The strict create/import path in `src/internal/api/automation/decode.go:98-107` already requires a single JSON value and rejects unknown fields, so toggles, routine installs, batch export, and backup-config writes have materially weaker transport contracts. Trailing or misspelled fields can be silently ignored before a toggle/install/export/config mutation, while oversized automation bodies can reach these routes. Nearby strict automation decoding demonstrates the canonical behavior: EOF after one object plus `DisallowUnknownFields`, plus a bounded 1 MiB reader.
- **chunk-0071.md:7** — `src/internal/api/backup/handler.go:51-86`, `src/internal/api/backup/handler.go:101-112`: The backup export path silently degrades incomplete backups: each table query can fail, each row `Scan` can fail, and `rows.Err()` is never checked, but the code still sets HTTP 200 and emits `_meta.tables` as if every table exported. `BackupStats` has the same silent-failure shape for row-count queries, leaving a failed count as `0`; therefore an interrupted export or stats read can be mistaken for a complete backup or an empty table. `ExportData` also checks no `rows.Err()` after iteration and even ignores the row scan error; this differs from the repository collectors in this chunk and risks truncation that masquerades as a valid backup artifact.
- **chunk-0072.md:3** — `src/internal/api/backup/restore_handler.go:74`, `src/internal/api/backup/restore_handler.go:104`, `src/internal/api/batterydegradation/certificate_handler.go:97`: These mutating/public JSON decoders call `json.Decoder.Decode` once and accept trailing JSON, unknown fields, and an unbounded body. A second object or unknown typo therefore does not invalidate the request; for public certificate verification it can also make malformed transport content appear as valid HTTP 200 processing input. The reviewed strict API handlers use unknown-field rejection and/or EOF/trailing-value checks (for example `src/internal/api/aicrossrule/handler.go:136-151`, `src/internal/api/aialerttune/handler.go:116-124`); these two backup writes and the certificate verifier bypass that transport contract.
- **chunk-0073.md:6** — `src/internal/api/fleetops/handler.go:110-127`, `src/internal/api/aipostcard/handler.go:114-123`, `src/internal/api/aisuggeo/handler.go:116-123`: The 64-KiB `decodeBody` wrapper uses `http.MaxBytesReader` but maps every decoder error, including `*http.MaxBytesError`, to HTTP 400 "invalid request body". An oversized JSON body that exceeds the advertised fleet-ops limit is therefore reported as an ordinary malformed body instead of HTTP 413, unlike canonical bounded handlers that explicitly detect the max-reader error. The same helper is used by every create/update body in the fleet-ops package, so all write routes inherit this status-code inconsistency. Adjacent canonical handlers detect/max-cap body errors and return HTTP 413; without `errors.As(err, *http.MaxBytesError)` (or equivalent) clients cannot distinguish a valid-size payload validation failure from an oversized request.
- **chunk-0073.md:7** — `src/internal/database/telemetry/fleet_error_repo.go:137-140`, `src/internal/api/fleettelemetry/error_handler.go:98-101`: `ReplaceErrorVINs` wraps an upsert transaction failure as `upsert error vin %s`, embedding the raw Fleet VIN in the returned error. `RefreshErrorVINs` then logs that error verbatim, so VIN (PII) appears in operational error logs even though project logging rules prohibit VINs. A later error in the refresh transaction can likewise expose every VIN supplied by the caller. The handler already maps repository failures to generic HTTP responses; remove VIN-bearing text and retain only a safe operation/database error context, with the VIN stored or represented by a non-identifying key if correlation is required.
- **chunk-0074.md:3** — `src/internal/api/chargeheatmap/handler.go:63-68`: The charging-heatmap vehicle ID parser accepts negative IDs because it checks only `strconv.ParseInt` success. The handler then queries charging session rows with `vehicle_id=-7`; the package test suite explicitly pins this as accepted behavior instead of rejecting it. Canonical positive-ID handlers in this snapshot (for example `chargeopt/handler.go:33-37`, `chargeautopilot/handler.go:35-42`) reject zero and negative values; accepting negative IDs can trigger unnecessary database work and inconsistent API validation.
- **chunk-0074.md:4** — `src/internal/api/chargeautopilot/handler.go:69-72,95-98`, `src/internal/api/chargeautopilot/run.go:71-74`, `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/handler.go:71-74,360-363`, `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/queue.go:155-158`: These JSON request decoders accept a valid first object followed by a second object or other trailing content because they call a single `Decode` and never consume/validate EOF. The valid-prefix body proceeds into profile, charge-plan, or queue validation instead of being rejected at the transport boundary. The same one-object decoder pattern is repeated across six handler methods in this chunk; package bodies therefore accept malformed trailing JSON where a strict JSON contract should return HTTP 400.
- **chunk-0074.md:5** — `src/internal/api/chargeautopilot/compute.go:76-82`: `parseReadyBy` validates hour/minute bounds but does not enforce the exact five-character `HH:MM` shape, unlike the adjacent `chargeplanner/queue.go:131-136` clock parser. Strings with trailing characters can potentially be accepted by `fmt.Sscanf`, producing a ready-by profile that downstream scheduling interprets despite invalid wire input. The two packages implement the same time-of-day contract with different edge validation; a shared exact clock parser would prevent malformed values from diverging between queue and autopilot flows.
- **chunk-0075.md:5** — `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/handler.go:71`, `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/handler.go:360`, `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/queue.go:155`: The three charge-planner POST handlers decode directly with `json.NewDecoder(r.Body).Decode`, with no body-size limit, strict unknown-field rejection, or EOF/trailing-JSON check. A large body is accepted into the decoder, and typos can silently leave required/defaulted fields zero or be ignored after a valid first JSON value. This is inconsistent with the neighboring bulk and guarded API patterns. Canonical bulk decoding in `src/internal/api/apibulk/helpers.go:67-76` uses `DisallowUnknownFields` and shared validation; nearby protected handlers use body caps and strict decoding (for example `src/internal/api/adminfeedback/handler.go:181-186`). Unbounded/lenient request parsing can permit resource use and produce misleading planner commands from misspelled transport fields.
- **chunk-0075.md:6** — `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/apply.go:63-65`: `ApplyPlanByID` returns success even when `planRepo.UpdateStatus(..., "scheduled", ...)` fails. It has already issued both vehicle commands, but leaves the database row in `draft`; callers receive HTTP 200 and the frontend can retry or display an applied schedule despite no durable status transition. The repository update is the transaction boundary between externally applied commands and persisted state (`src/internal/database/charging/plan_repo.go:109-113`); ignoring this error means the status and Tesla state diverge permanently unless a later operation notices it. Adjacent command failures are propagated and mapped, unlike this post-command persistence failure.
- **chunk-0075.md:7** — `src/internal/api/charging/variance.go:58-66`: `ComputeBillVariance` leaves percentage fields at zero when the measured denominator is zero and therefore a missing/empty baseline can be classified as reconciled. In particular, zero measured energy/cost plus nonzero invoice totals skips both percentage calculations, so the switch sees `0%` and can return `"reconciled"` and claim agreement. The function promises to reconcile measured packs against invoices, and it explicitly has a `"missing_data"` mode; this branch should not manufacture zero deltas. A vehicle with zero measured sessions/energy is materially different from equal measured and invoiced totals and risks a false green reconciliation result.
- **chunk-0075.md:8** — `src/internal/api/chargetelem/handler.go:62-64`, `src/internal/api/chargetelem/handler.go:94-96`: Both charging-telemetry endpoints reject only zero IDs after `ParseInt`; negative `vehicle_id=-42` passes validation and reaches the state reader. `src/internal/api/charging/handler.go:133-136` and the bill-variance handler at `src/internal/api/charging/variance.go:113-118` require positive IDs. Negative IDs are invalid transport input and should be rejected consistently before querying state, rather than treated as a cross-tenant/undefined lookup key.
- **chunk-0076.md:5** — `src/internal/api/climate/handler.go:80-82`, `src/internal/api/climate/handler.go:118-120`: Both climate history and latest-state handlers reject only zero IDs; negative `vehicle_id` values pass validation and are sent to `StateReader.Timeline` / `LiveStateReader.LiveState`. This is invalid input that should be rejected consistently before querying state. Nearby positive-ID validation appears in `src/internal/api/chartannotation/handler.go:76-80`, `src/internal/api/comfort/handler.go:203-206`, and other handlers in this catalog; negative IDs can be treated as undefined lookup keys instead of returning `400`.
- **chunk-0076.md:6** — `src/internal/api/comfort/handler.go:214-238`: `evaluateOne` reserves/logs the event UID before `startClimate` succeeds. If either Tesla command fails, the UID remains recorded and every later evaluation pass skips the event, so a transient API failure permanently prevents the configured preconditioning from being retried. The comment at `handler.go:214-216` explicitly uses `LogRun` as a reservation, but that reservation is currently coupled to the externally visible outcome; there is no status/rollback path.
- **chunk-0076.md:7** — `src/internal/api/comfort/handler.go:256-259`: `startClimate` returns `nil` when `GetByID` succeeds but returns a nil vehicle with no error. The caller then reports preconditioning as started/logged even though no Tesla command was issued. `startClimate` is used by both immediate preconditioning and event evaluation (`handler.go:149-169`, `238-244`); nil must be treated as an error, consistent with callers that require an actual vehicle VIN.
