Positive-ID validation appears in adjacent entity handlers (for example `src/internal/api/vampiredrain/handler.go:66-72` and `src/internal/api/tripsdetail/handler.go:88-93`); accepting negative keys can leak into DB lookups and state queries before a 404/400 boundary.
- **chunk-0104.md:7** — `src/internal/api/vehicleaccess/handler.go:96-101,117-122,208-213,240-245`: Vehicle-access handlers validate only path-int parsing. `resolveVehicle`, list, refresh, and invitation methods can accept negative vehicle IDs; `RemoveDriver` additionally accepts negative `share_user_id` values because it rejects only zero. The resulting repository/Tesla calls receive invalid IDs. Nearby canonical handlers reject `<= 0`, and the share user ID is an external identifier only meaningful as positive; negative values should fail before vehicle/Tesla lookup or mutation.
- **chunk-0104.md:8** — `src/internal/api/vehicleaccess/handler.go:208-210`: `RemoveDriver` decodes one body with no size limit, unknown-field rejection, or EOF/trailing-value check. A typoed/extra field is ignored, and a valid first object followed by trailing JSON can still trigger an outbound driver removal. Strict mutation patterns in the same API family (for example `src/internal/api/vehiclesettings/handler.go:132-147`) reject unknown fields and enforce one-document/size-limited decoding before persistence or external mutation.
- **chunk-0105.md:5** — `src/internal/api/vehicle/handler.go:138,158,201,269,371`, `src/internal/api/vehicle/silence.go:93`: URL vehicle IDs are parsed only for syntax with `apiparams.URLParamInt64`; these handlers do not reject zero or negative IDs. Negative IDs therefore reach repo/state lookups, the signal timeline reader, and command-adjacent Tesla operations instead of failing at the HTTP boundary. The positive-ID contract is repeated across the package/API; `Get`, `Delete`, `Positions`, `CurrentState`, and `Wake` can each accept negative path IDs, while `Silence` can query the state reader with one. The vehicle package's own tests cover non-numeric IDs, but do not pin the positive-ID edge case.
- **chunk-0105.md:6** — `src/internal/api/vehicleconfig/handler.go:73-75,128-130`: Both vehicle-config query handlers reject parse failures and zero (`vehicleID == 0`) but allow negative IDs. Negative IDs are passed to `StateReader.Timeline`/live state and can produce synthetic empty or cache/mapping behavior from an impossible entity key. The neighboring query contract treats vehicle IDs as required positive values in handlers such as `aianomaly`, `aialert`, and `aimlrange`; these two endpoints should validate the same invariant.
- **chunk-0105.md:7** — `src/internal/api/vehicleaccess/handler.go:208-213`: `RemoveDriver` decodes the request with a plain `json.Decoder`, checks only `ShareUserID == 0`, and does not require a single JSON document, disallow unknown fields, or cap the body. Negative share-user IDs pass validation and trigger `RemoveVehicleDriver`, while unknown/extra fields and trailing values are silently accepted or ignored. The strict, bounded `decodeLimitedJSON` pattern in `src/internal/api/vehicleinfo/handler.go:733-759` uses `DisallowUnknownFields` plus EOF validation; the vehicle-access mutation has only a zero-value check. A misspelled or trailing request can therefore invoke an external write using a defaulted or unintended identifier instead of being rejected.
- **chunk-0106.md:5** — `src/internal/api/watch/handler.go:191-198,213-225,358-363`: The watch command mutation decoder accepts one permissive `json.Decoder` value without a size limit, `DisallowUnknownFields`, or an EOF/trailing-document check; it also treats only `vehicle_id == 0` as fallback and therefore passes negative IDs to `GetByID`, after query endpoints have already established `<= 0` as invalid. Unknown fields can silently default `vehicle_id=0` or alter intent, while a valid first object followed by another JSON value still dispatches. The same watch package rejects negative IDs in `resolveWatchVehicleID`, and adjacent mutation handlers use bounded/strict decoding; this command path is the exception and is security-sensitive because it reaches Tesla Fleet commands.
- **chunk-0106.md:6** — `src/internal/api/watch/handler.go:288-345`: `queryWatchSummary` never sets the exported `State` field. Live summaries therefore return `"state":""`; the complication path also turns every live state into `stateEmoji("unknown")` even when cache data contains `ChargeState`/other live state. The fallback summary explicitly reports `"unknown"` and complication tests cover live battery/range, but no test asserts live state, so the live cache success path loses the vehicle state consistently while `ChargeState` is already read for charging detection.
- **chunk-0106.md:7** — `src/internal/api/weberrors/handler.go:134-145`: The web-error ingest decoder enforces a 32 KiB body limit and rejects unknown fields, but it does not enforce a single JSON document: after decoding one valid object it performs no EOF check. A valid report followed by `{}` or trailing malformed JSON is accepted as one report and increments browser-error metrics/logging. Neighboring strict handlers in `src/internal/api/fleetops/handler.go:111-122`, `src/internal/api/benchmark/handler.go:283-287`, and exported-job decoding perform an EOF check, so malformed multi-document bodies are handled differently on equivalent mutation surfaces.
- **chunk-0106.md:8** — `src/internal/api/vehiclestates/handler.go:91-99`: The vehicle-states `days` parameter has no upper bound. For sufficiently large positive integers, `time.Duration(days) * 24 * time.Hour` in `windowFor` can overflow, producing a reversed or otherwise corrupted query window rather than a 400. Tests explicitly exercise small day values only and the package comment says “no max-days cap”; the parser's `Atoi` otherwise accepts the full platform integer range while downstream time arithmetic is limited to `time.Duration`'s roughly 290-year range.
- **chunk-0107.md:5** — `src/internal/api/webvitals/handler.go:116-126,128-145`: The Web Vitals ingest handler performs one permissive-document decode followed by a single `Decode`; it does not require EOF after the first JSON object. A body containing a valid batch followed by a second JSON value, trailing garbage, or trailing whitespace-plus-object is accepted and recorded as one request even though the wire contract allows only one document. This bypasses the strict body-contract boundary established by `DisallowUnknownFields`: unknown-field rejection catches malformed schemas only inside the first value, while valid-prefix extra data is ignored. The same JSON decoder pattern appears alongside public ingest/mutation handlers whose strict contract should reject one-document violations.
- **chunk-0107.md:6** — `src/internal/automation/trigger/webhook.go:39-62`, `src/internal/api/webhookreceiver/handler.go:36-77`, `src/internal/api/webhookreceiver/handler_test.go:88-224`: The public webhook receiver is a permanent nonfunctional stub: every valid automation token lookup succeeds only to return `ErrWebhookNotFound`, because `HandleWebhook` never validates payload/signature or evaluates the automation engine and explicitly says the typed automation runtime is unavailable. The HTTP tests also assert `404` for valid payloads, empty bodies, missing signatures, invalid JSON, and even valid-looking HMAC headers, so the public route advertises token authentication but cannot execute or accept any webhook kind. Router wiring exposes it publicly as `POST /api/v1/automations/webhook/{token}` (`src/internal/api/router.go:2478-2487`) and the token-specific rate-limit key is built from the same route (`src/internal/api/webhookreceiver/handler.go:105-110`), while no outbound signature or payload processing exists in the receiver/trigger implementation. Unknown, disabled, malformed, and available webhook requests all converge on the same 404 behavior, so callers cannot diagnose automation availability and no automation is ever fired.
- **chunk-0108.md:5** — `src/internal/apilog/async.go:102-109`, `src/internal/apilog/async.go:64-74`: `Shutdown` closes the async logger's channel while `Enqueue` is still allowed to inspect/check and send concurrently. The channel can therefore be closed between the `closed.Load()` check and `select` send, causing a send-on-closed-channel panic in a request or worker goroutine. This contradicts the package's concurrent-shutdown contract and is not defended by a recover path in the API caller. The normal post-shutdown path is safe only after `closed.Load()` becomes true; an in-flight enqueue racing the `close(a.ch)` call is the exposed window. The package tests cover a subsequent enqueue and concurrent ordinary enqueues separately, but do not exercise an enqueue racing `Shutdown`, so this crash remains unpinned.
- **chunk-0108.md:6** — `src/internal/platform/httputil/logging.go:174-193`, `src/internal/platform/httputil/logging.go:52-58`: `captureAndRestoreBody` does not restore bytes read before an `io.ReadAll` error. It returns the original body positioned after the partial read, and the callers leave the request/response body in that advanced position when capture fails. For an outbound request the sender can then transmit only the unread suffix; for a response the caller can receive only the unread suffix while transport captures neither the partial prefix nor the lost bytes. Both request and response paths use this same helper. Existing truncation and disabled-capture tests cover successful reads, but not a partial-read error, so a transport can silently corrupt the byte stream only when the upstream body returns a read error.
- **chunk-0109.md:5** — `src/internal/app/advancedintelligencesvc/twin_firmware.go:251-267`, `src/internal/app/advancedintelligencesvc/survival_security_charging.go:144-185,205-227,324-377`, `src/internal/app/advancedintelligencesvc/planning.go:305-315`: Several advanced-intelligence read methods accept any `vehicleID` without the positive-ID validation used by adjacent `JourneyAssurance`, `ResiliencePlan`, `TCO`, `FederatedStatus`, and the causal APIs. `FirmwareCanary`, `ComponentSurvival`, `RoadHazards`, `BehavioralSentinel`, and `ChargingForensics` therefore pass negative or zero IDs into source repositories and signal readers; the same service file validates `<= 0` for mutating/write and other read endpoints. Repeated omission spans five exported methods. An invalid vehicle key can reach bounded evidence queries, widen externally observable service behavior, and produce synthetic quality/empty responses instead of `ErrInvalidInput` at the service boundary.
- **chunk-0109.md:6** — `src/internal/app/actioncentersvc/service.go:122-143`: The Action Center summary is accumulated before the priority, source-feature, and state filters are applied. `List` increments summary counters for every generated recommendation, then skips items only after the summary has already counted them. Filtering `/action-center` by priority, source, or state returns `Total`, `Items`, and pagination based on the filtered set but a `Summary` derived from the unfiltered set. Tests do not assert summary consistency under filtered requests, so this can expose misleading counts while the caller still sees only filtered rows.
- **chunk-0110.md:5** — `src/internal/app/chargingsvc/service.go:38-115`, `src/internal/adapter/postgres/charging_repository.go:25-88`, `src/internal/adapter/postgres/queries/charging.go:14-78`: The charging application service advances a real FSM, but the PostgreSQL adapter discards the FSM state and several aggregate fields. `Save` persists only session IDs plus battery/energy/cost/time columns, while reads synthesize `fsm_state='completed'`, `sub_fsm_state=''`, and `charger_connected=false`; `HandleEvent` therefore loads an already-completed session after any subsequent lookup and cannot start, retry, fail, or otherwise transition it. The repository also has no way to persist charger connection, making the `CanStartCharging` guard non-persistent after a reconnecting session is reloaded. The service and fakes agree on an in-memory state model and tests cover it, but `mockChargingRepo.Save` copies the whole struct while production `GetByID` reads a synthetic hardcoded state. Existing service test cases pass only because the test adapter does not expose this production mismatch; list and date-range reads use the same synthetic projection.
- **chunk-0110.md:6** — `src/internal/app/chargingsvc/service.go:30-43`, `src/internal/domain/charging/guards.go:11-13`, `src/internal/domain/charging/fsm.go:29-33`, `src/internal/domain/charging/fsm_test.go:188-202`: `setupFSM` registers the start-charge guard but never registers `CanComplete`. Consequently `EventComplete` reaches `StateCharging -> StateCompleting -> StateCompleted` without validating that energy was added and that the ending SoC is not lower than the starting SoC. The guard exists in the domain and has its own unit test, yet it is absent from the production engine's guard registration, so production completion events can mark invalid sessions complete while the guard suite gives false coverage. The same two-state completion path appears in `NewChargingFSM`, but no call connects `CanComplete` to its transition; `sub_fsm.go` has terminal handling but also does not add this business guard. Repository writes then serialize the aggregate regardless of the guard's result, allowing invalid completion data to enter the domain/application boundary.
- **chunk-0111.md:5** — `src/internal/app/fleetstatesvc/cache.go:245-275`, `src/internal/app/fleetstatesvc/service.go:204-214`: Query normalization converts every-invalid-ID requests (`VehicleIDs: [-1]`, zero, and combinations where no positive ID remains) into `out.VehicleIDs == nil`, but an explicit query and the whole-roster query are intentionally different. `cacheKey(normalize(q))` then renders the normalized nil list as `ids=*`, and `selectVehicles` also treats nil as “whole fleet,” so an explicit invalid/negative-only selection can return every vehicle. The cache-key contract explicitly distinguishes “the whole roster” from explicit sets at `cache.go:257-272`, but normalization destroys that distinction at `cache.go:294-319`. Existing tests cover sorted/duplicate valid IDs, pagination, and positive filtering, but not invalid-only lists; negative IDs are allowed into the query and become a fleet-wide read instead of an empty selection.
- **chunk-0111.md:6** — `src/internal/signal/redis_cache.go:458-466`, `src/internal/app/fleetstatesvc/summary.go:198-204`, `src/internal/service/current_state.go:220`: `IsLiveSignalFresh` requires only `age <= 2 minutes`; it does not reject future timestamps (`age < 0`). A clock-skewed or malformed live value observed seconds, hours, or even years in the future is therefore classified fresh by this shared oracle. Fleet Posture uses the oracle before counting a vehicle as trusted, and current-state assembly uses the same result to assign `FreshnessFresh`; one synthetic/future Redis/signal-log timestamp can therefore produce a live operational claim while stale/zero timestamps are handled by separate checks. Additive equality/boundary tests should require `age >= 0 && age <= threshold`.
- **chunk-0111.md:7** — `src/internal/app/exportsvc/service.go:62-91`, `src/internal/app/vehiclesvc/service.go:112-147`, `src/internal/app/tripsvc/service.go:63-105`, `src/internal/app/chargingsvc/service.go:87-113`: Export FSM state transitions are persisted to the repository, then the transition history write is allowed to fail independently. If history recording fails, the API reports an error even though the aggregate already reached the new state; retrying the same event then hits the FSM as an invalid transition from that state. This partial-commit pattern is duplicated across four sibling application services. The job/session/vehicle/trip state should not be reported as transition failure after the Save succeeded, and history persistence needs an atomic/compensating transaction or deferred retry contract rather than an immediate state-save followed by a best-effort-independent history insert.
- **chunk-0112.md:5** — `src/internal/app/notificationsvc/service.go:54-104`, `src/internal/domain/notification/fsm.go:13-32`: Notification delivery has no crash-safe retry path. `Send` changes `pending` to `sending`, sends externally, then either confirms or fails; a process crash after the sending transition leaves the aggregate permanently in `sending`, because the FSM defines no `sending -> sending/confirm/fail` recovery transition from a fresh retry call and `Send` itself cannot accept a notification already in that state. Failed notifications also require the unimplemented `EventRetry` path before `EventSend`, so no service method increments `RetryCount` or moves them back to sending. The same package test harness only covers one uninterrupted send and send failure (`src/internal/app/notificationsvc/service_test.go:152-229`); it never covers restart recovery or retrying a failed row. The FSM therefore can strand records after either an external-send crash or a later worker retry, contradicting the available `retrying` state and `RetryCount` field.
- **chunk-0112.md:6** — `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/warranty.go:288-367`, `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/warranty.go:184-267`: Warranty coverage with an estimated battery-capacity floor breach is reported as active even when the capacity floor has already been reached. `projectCoverage` sets `BindingLimit = "capacity"` and records `CapacityFloorBreachAt`, but does not move `ProjectedExpiryAt` to that breach and `coverage.Active` only checks the time and optional distance limits. The overview can consequently show an expired capacity binding as active/expiring and keep the claim window open past the breach. `buildReadiness` likewise treats capacity evidence as a gate but does not mark the claim window closed for a capacity breach (`src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/warranty.go:393-451`), while `coverageNarrative` reports the capacity binding as the prospective expiry despite `Active` not incorporating it. Users can receive inconsistent claim readiness for battery warranties.
- **chunk-0112.md:7** — `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/modeltrust.go:59-105`, `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/modeltrust.go:241-388`, `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/modeltrust.go:470-495`: `RecordPrediction` validates that the low bound is not above the high bound only when both are present; it does not reject one-sided intervals, and it does not validate either bound as finite. `buildCalibration` and interval coverage also require both bounds before scoring. A one-sided or non-finite interval is persisted by `CreatePrediction`/storage (`src/internal/port/ownershipintel/records.go:191-194`) and later silently omitted from interval coverage, calibration bins, and `InInterval` even though it was accepted at the write boundary. `ObservedValue` is similarly split across readback and scoring in `modeltrust.go`, so malformed interval semantics are easy to miss in reported trust.
- **chunk-0112.md:8** — `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/tariff.go:37-113`, `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/tariff.go:434-469`: Tariff creation validates each price band's own time/rate bounds but never rejects overlapping weekday/time/season bands. `selectRate` then chooses the first matching record for a settlement slice, with database/record insertion order deciding the price. The tiered/time-of-use/demand selectors in the same file have deterministic first-match semantics, but no uniqueness or overlap invariant exists in `CreateTariff`, so two rows can price the same 15-minute interval differently depending on ordering. This can produce materially wrong annual cost and `LoadShiftSavingMinor`, especially for overlapping TOU bands.
- **chunk-0113.md:6** — `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/warranty.go:327-354,359-369`: The capacity-floor projection is inconsistent with the coverage lifecycle: when capacity retention reaches the floor, the code sets `BindingLimit="capacity"` and `CapacityFloorBreachAt`, but never moves `ProjectedExpiryAt` to that breach and does not include it in `Active`. Capacity-exceeded coverage consequently remains `active`, with time/distance expiry in `ProjectedExpiryAt` and a contradictory `"capacity"` binding, while the frontend displays the breach separately. A record with a capacity floor and breached retention has no single consistent projected-expiry contract; status/readiness and claim-window calculations use `ProjectedExpiryAt`, but that value excludes the exact capacity condition represented in `CapacityFloorBreachAt`. The same projection function has six readiness gates and must select the earliest binding limit consistently across time, distance, and capacity.
- **chunk-0114.md:5** — `src/internal/app/sciencesvc/weather.go:52-101,111`: The weather archive cache is keyed only by rounded coordinates and UTC date, while each drive fetch is performed for that drive's exact `[start,end]` interval. Reusing a cached response for a later drive on the same day suppresses a fetch for the later interval, so `nearestHour` may find no sample and mark the weather point missing even though hours for that later drive could be available from the archive. The cache also has no coverage metadata, so a partial/earlier successful fetch can be mistaken for coverage of the whole day. `nearestHour` is the sole consumer of the cached slice; a bad cache hit at `src/internal/app/sciencesvc/weather.go:111` returns `weather_archive_hour` without retrying. A second drive at the same location/date is the directly affected repeated path and can alternate a successful/missing result based on ordering.
- **chunk-0114.md:6** — `src/internal/app/tripsvc/service.go:48-49,84,105`, `src/internal/app/vehiclesvc/service.go:52-53,102,132,146`, `src/internal/app/chargingsvc/service.go:48,67,76` (charging file outside the worker chunk): Trip, vehicle, and charging application services stamp persisted lifecycle timestamps with `time.Now()` instead of UTC as required by the project Go time convention. Although `time.Now()` carries an instant, it stores a location-bearing value and can produce non-UTC representations and inconsistent JSON/database behavior when the process runs outside UTC; using a common UTC timestamp also matters for ordering and cross-service transition records. The defect repeats across all lifecycle write paths in these three services, while nearby domain/repository serialization and the synthetic/wiring tests do not normalize them before persistence. Each service can coexist in a deployment and emit differently represented timestamps for the same event instant.
- **chunk-0115.md:5** — `src/internal/arch/arch_test.go:25-31,377-413,520-529,598-605,650-657,705-712`: Every architecture graph test calls `packages.Load` and treats only the returned top-level `error` as fatal; it never checks `packages.Package.Errors`. A package can fail type-checking or loading while `Load` returns `nil`, so its imports may be absent and forbidden-edge/domain/port/adapter/handler tests can produce false passes instead of architecture failures. The same package-loading pattern is repeated across the forbidden-edge, domain, models, port, adapter, and handler-v1 tests. Canonical package loading should inspect package errors before evaluating edges, so an unresolvable package cannot silently bypass the gate.
- **chunk-0115.md:6** — `src/internal/arch/rules.go:281-288`, `src/internal/arch/arch_test.go:375-413`: `DomainAllowedInternalImports` claims to be the closed internal-import authority for domain packages, but `TestDomainPurity` does not reference it and instead hardcodes `"internal/domain"` plus `"internal/domain/"`. Updating the exported allowlist alone would change no enforcement behavior and could leave code/tests visually consistent while allowing a rule to drift from the actual test. The test imports a domain allow-model concept (`DomainAllowedInternalImports`) that is otherwise unused, while focused and forbidden-edge enforcement duplicate this package boundary. The allowlist should be consumed by the focused test or removed if it is not configuration; otherwise maintenance edits cannot remain aligned.
- **chunk-0116.md:5** — `src/internal/audit/recorder.go:188-190,272-316`, `src/internal/audit/recorder_test.go:13-44`: The tamper-evident hash canonicalizes actor, category, action, entity, detail, before/after, trace ID, timestamp, and success, but omits `IP` and `UserAgent` even though both are Event fields and persisted audit columns. `VerifyChain` also does not select or canonicalize them, so changing either privacy/attribution field leaves the row hash and `VerifyChain` result intact. The verifier and recorder are a second copy of the same canonical shape: both must include every persisted Event field, or hash-chain claims do not cover the full row. The existing deterministic/tamper tests cover EntityType but never IP or user-agent mutation.
- **chunk-0116.md:6** — `src/internal/audit/recorder.go:188-211`: Concurrent successful `Record` calls advance `prevRow` before insertion, and any later insert failure resets `prevRow` to the failed call's old value rather than only rolling back its own head. If two calls overlap, call A can fail after call B has advanced the head; A then overwrites B's chain state, so the in-memory head diverges from the already inserted hashes and future rows link to an earlier hash. This is the same recorder's synchronization flaw across concurrent callers: the mutex protects assignment, but not compute-plus-`Exec`, and rollback is unconditional instead of conditional on the current head. It can corrupt a live chain without returning a mismatched database insert for the later call.
- **chunk-0116.md:7** — `src/internal/audit/recorder.go:128-137,172-177`: `New` defaults to a deny-all redactor, but `Record` never validates `ev.Category` with the package's existing `Category.Validate`; it stores and chains arbitrary category strings. An empty/unknown category can therefore enter the fixed category vocabulary (`auth`, `admin`, `data`, `config`, `security`) documented by `Category`, and the viewer's distinct-category filters will expose arbitrary values. `Category.Validate` exists in the same package and is covered by tests, but the production write boundary never calls it. Required Event fields are validated while category, despite having a fixed compile-time review contract, is not.
- **chunk-0117.md:5** — `src/internal/auth/session_tracker.go:125-168,210-218`: The valid-session branch updates `last_seen_at` but never refreshes the browser's `Max-Age`, so the `teslasync_session` cookie expires 30 days after its original mint even for continuously active users. The surrounding comments promise the opposite: active users should effectively not see expiration. The mint path independently sets `MaxAge` to `CookieMaxAge` at `src/internal/auth/session_tracker.go:210-218`, while the established-cookie branch at `125-168` only bumps DB activity and never emits another cookie, creating behavior drift from the documented rolling-session contract. A 30-day browser expiry silently revokes the device despite the DB session remaining active.
- **chunk-0117.md:6** — `src/internal/auth/impersonation.go:208-232`: Cookie expiry validation accepts a claim exactly at its `ExpiresAt` boundary: it checks `s.now().After(claim.ExpiresAt)` rather than rejecting a current time equal to or after expiry. Minted cookies created with `expiresAt = now().Add(15m)` can remain valid at the exact instant and precision granularity of expiry. The TTL boundary is documented as a 15-minute upper bound in the Mint/Verify comments, but tests cover expiration only after advancing beyond TTL, not equality or the store's configured clock. Reusing the exact boundary can extend the impersonation session beyond the stated TTL when clocks have coarse resolution.
- **chunk-0118.md:3** — `src/internal/automation/action/variable.go:22-24,104-124`, `src/internal/database/automation/variable_repo.go:23-38`: Automation-variable reads are not vehicle-scoped even though the executor receives a vehicle ID and the repository stores it in `vehicle_id`. `Execute` calls `Get(ctx, key)` without the vehicle ID, while the repository defines `Get` as a global lookup by unique `key`; a vehicle-local value can therefore be overwritten in-place and read by another vehicle automation. The same key's row also changes `vehicle_id` on each `Set`, contradicting the `AutomationVariable.IsGlobal` scope contract. The SET path passes `vehicleID` and the model/migration expose nullable per-row vehicle scope, but the GET interface has no scope parameter and every lookup is `WHERE key = $1`. A key used by one vehicle scope can expose stale or cross-vehicle previous/current values.
- **chunk-0118.md:4** — `src/internal/automation/condition/cooldown.go:24-42,49-69`: Cooldown validation has no upper bound and `EvaluateCooldown` multiplies the caller-provided `int` by `time.Minute` as a `time.Duration`. Very large positive minute values overflow duration arithmetic and can wrap to a negative cooldown; the comparison `elapsed >= cooldownDuration` then treats the automation as always eligible, bypassing its configured cooldown. Nearby bounded duration logic in `src/internal/automation/action/wait.go:14-18,62-68` caps waits at 3600 seconds. Cooldown has only `minutes > 0`, so a programmatic/decoded oversized value avoids the positive check and can invalidate the safety condition.
- **chunk-0119.md:5** — `src/internal/automation/engine.go:219-220`, `src/internal/automation/engine_execution.go:95-107`, `src/internal/automation/action/chain.go:112-122,142-145`, `src/internal/automation/action/command.go:205-228`: Vehicle-scoped automations lose their configured vehicle context during action execution. `Evaluate` builds the action chain and calls `ChainExecutor.Execute(..., vehicle=nil, ...)` even though `models.AutomationFull.VehicleID` is available; the chain consequently passes a nil vehicle ID, and `CommandExecutor.executeCommandConfig` interprets that nil as “fleet-wide” and enumerates every vehicle. A scoped automation can therefore issue its command to unrelated vehicles instead of its configured vehicle. The runtime typed-action test only asserts that a typed executor was called and never pins or checks the vehicle ID, so this branch is unverified. This differs from the action-chain contract documented in `chain.go`: non-nil vehicles mean one vehicle, nil means fleet-wide. `engine.go` already declares and stores `a.VehicleID`; it must resolve that vehicle or pass the ID into chain execution. The same misrouting affects other vehicle-aware executors such as notify and variable actions.
- **chunk-0119.md:6** — `src/cmd/automation-worker/main.go:198-204`, `src/internal/automation/engine.go:38-51,112-115`, `src/internal/automation/engine_evaluation.go:31-42,72-86`: The automation worker constructs the engine without `WithStateProvider` and without `WithPlaceProvider`. Consequently typed signal/geofence conditions always become `unknown`, and geofence/signal condition evaluation never reads live state or place rows; the engine treats those unknown results as unmet, skipping actions. Existing production state and place providers may exist conceptually (`RedisStateProvider`, `PlaceProvider`) but neither is attached by the worker constructor, so this is not merely an inactive optional path. The engine test suite does attach `WithStateProvider`, but the production wiring in the worker does not, allowing tests to pass while deployed automations with signal/geofence conditions are silently suppressed.
- **chunk-0119.md:7** — `src/internal/automation/condition/location.go:107-126`, `src/internal/automation/engine_evaluation.go:229-234`: Geofence distance is implemented twice with two different formulas: legacy location decoding/evaluation uses spherical-law-of-cosines (`earthRadiusM` and `Acos`), while typed automation conditions use haversine (`Sin(dLat/2)`, etc.). Both are intended to assess the same geofence boundary, but they can disagree at boundary precision and on nontrivial distances because the formulas are approximate rather than identical. The location file explicitly says its result matches the SQL formula, while the typed engine does not share it; duplicated domain logic is parameterizable through an existing/common distance helper. Boundary tests in `location_test.go` are marked approximate and do not assert a precise same-result contract with the typed evaluator.
- **chunk-0119.md:8** — `src/internal/automation/condition/variable_check.go:38-53,77-110`, `src/internal/automation/engine_evaluation.go:237-305`, `src/internal/automation/engine_evaluation_list_test.go:5-42`: Numeric comparison paths accept `NaN`/`Inf` more broadly than their tests defend. `DecodeVariableCheckSpec` calls `strconv.ParseFloat` for ordering thresholds and accepts `NaN`, `+Inf`, and `-Inf`; they then participate in comparisons where all results are commonly false or architecture-dependent. The `in` branch of typed signal-condition evaluation correctly rejects `NaN` and infinities, but ordinary numeric variable ordering has no equivalent guard, and ordinary `compareFloat64` numeric comparisons in the engine also permit non-finite values if supplied through a model or JSON number-adjacent path. The in-list implementation is an explicit second copy of numeric validation and already rejects non-finite values, demonstrating the intended domain rule; the variable parser and typed comparison normal path do not consistently enforce it. Invalid thresholds can create persistently unmet/false conditions instead of failing configuration validation.
- **chunk-0119.md:9** — `src/internal/automation/engine_evaluation.go:31-42,72-86,94-110,229-234`, `src/internal/automation/engine.go:186-220`: Typed condition evaluation performs place, automation, history, and state lookups with `context.Background()` instead of the evaluation context, bypassing trigger/request cancellation and deadlines. `evaluateConditions` receives only the automation and `now`, so every provider call is detached from `Engine.Evaluate(ctx, ...)`; a worker shutdown or cancelled trigger therefore cannot stop Redis/place/history queries. This violates the project context-propagation rule for downstream calls. Triggered evaluations already carry a real context, but it is discarded in every typed provider lookup and replaced with a fresh background context.
- **chunk-0120.md:3** — `src/internal/automation/trigger/geofence.go:212-265,337-368`, `src/internal/api/automation/decode.go:352-367`, `src/internal/models/automation_step_trigger.go:32-36`, `src/internal/api/automation/handler.go:1015-1025`: Geofence dwell triggers accept and expose a caller-selected `dwell_minutes`, but the runtime never consumes it. The trigger loop reads `DwellMinutes` only to decide whether to schedule a timer, then `startDwellTimer` always uses the fixed package constant of 5 minutes. A `dwell_minutes=30` configuration therefore fires after 5 minutes, while a positive value is accepted by API validation and may be persisted/hydrated as distinct trigger data. The API contract, model, and handler all represent a configured dwell duration, but the runtime has only `dwellDuration=5*time.Minute`; this creates incorrect trigger timing and divergence between the automation configuration and its execution.
- **chunk-0120.md:4** — `src/internal/automation/trigger/compare.go:1-55,75-99`, `src/internal/automation/trigger/signal_test.go:54`, `src/internal/models/automation_step_trigger.go:11-17`, `src/internal/api/automation/decode.go:342-345`, `src/internal/automation/presets/preset_test.go:148`: Signal-trigger operators `crossed_above` and `crossed_below` are accepted by the model/API schema and explicitly allowed by preset validation, but `compareTypedValues` has no implementation for them and `signalTriggerMatches` always falls through to a false comparison result. Such automations therefore can never fire, despite being valid configuration. The schema and decoder advertise both operators, and tests enumerate one unsupported case, but the evaluator's supported operation switch only handles equality/relational numeric comparisons; the accepted trigger vocabulary is broader than runtime behavior.
- **chunk-0120.md:5** — `src/internal/automation/redis_state_provider.go:31-48`: `GetVehicleState` maps battery state by treating `BatteryLevel == 0` as “missing” before falling back to `Soc`: after parsing `BatteryLevel`, any later `Soc` value replaces the state with `int(Soc)` whenever the parsed battery level is exactly zero. A legitimate zero-percent battery reading (or an imprecise zero due to a missing string/int type) is therefore overwritten by a potentially different/stale `Soc` value, preventing the state-check engine from seeing the actual zero state. `BatteryLevel` and `Soc` are separate Redis signal keys; the fallback condition conflates a valid zero with absence, an edge that matters for charging/safety conditions and differs from pointer/null-aware state semantics.
- **chunk-0121.md:3** — `src/internal/api/automation/decode.go:352-362`, `src/internal/models/automation_step_trigger.go:31-36`, `src/internal/automation/trigger/geofence.go:154-244`: Geofence trigger validation rejects the documented/runtime event values `"leave"` and `"both"` even though the runtime explicitly handles both and the model comment lists both plus `"leave"`. The API vocabulary and evaluator therefore disagree; only `"enter"`, `"exit"`, and `"dwell"` can be created despite runtime support for transitional aliases. The same event vocabulary must be consistent across `validateAutomationTriggerGeofence`, the CTI model contract, and `OnPositionUpdate`; otherwise valid automation definitions represented by the model/runtime are unreachable through API decode, while the runtime retains legacy code paths.
- **chunk-0121.md:4** — `src/internal/api/automation/decode.go:358-362`, `src/internal/automation/trigger/geofence.go:82-86,209-236,347-368`: A positive `dwell_minutes` request is accepted only for the dwell event, but it is never stored in the typed geofence model and the runtime ignores it. Every accepted dwell trigger is scheduled for the hard-coded five minutes (`dwellDuration`) rather than the configured duration. `AutomationStepTriggerGeofence.DwellMinutes` is tagged `db:"-"`, and `startDwellTimer` receives the field but never converts it to a duration; tests assert the platform constant instead of honoring configuration. A requested 10-minute dwell would fire at 5 minutes, and the HTTP payload/storage contract is misleading.
- **chunk-0121.md:5** — `src/internal/api/automation/decode.go:334-349`, `src/internal/models/automation_step_trigger.go:15-23`, `src/internal/automation/trigger/signal.go:84-93`, `src/internal/automation/trigger/compare.go:22-69`: Typed signal triggers advertise and accept `"crossed_above"`/`"crossed_below"` as valid operators, but `signalTriggerMatches` routes them into `compareTypedValues`, which only implements numeric comparisons `= != > >= < <=` and returns false for crossed operators. The model/decode contract promises two operator values that can never fire through the evaluator, so persisted automations silently become dead rules. Supporting crossing semantics requires prior-state tracking; merely classifying these as unsupported would be safer than accepting them as threshold triggers.
- **chunk-0123.md:3** — `src/internal/backuprestore/restore.go:248-255,261-283`: Setting `session_replication_role = replica` disables PostgreSQL constraint triggers, including foreign-key checks, during the import. The code then restores and commits rows based only on dependency order and row counts; it does not validate referential integrity. A partial or corrupted artifact can therefore restore orphaned child rows (for example a child present while its parent table is omitted), and `DatabaseImported` can still become true after commit. The ordering logic models only parent dependencies among the tables included in `verified.RestoredData`, while the artifact is not required to contain all parent tables, and critical-table verification checks counts only after commit. Disabling FK enforcement creates a restore that can be internally inconsistent without detection.
- **chunk-0123.md:4** — `src/internal/backuprestore/restore.go:279-305`: The scratch import transaction is committed before critical-table verification runs. If a post-commit count query fails or an allowed critical table is empty, `Run` returns a failed result but the destructive reset/import has already been persisted; `DatabaseImported` and `OK` remain false. The same `Result` method promises a transactional import/recovery drill and its rollback test only proves failures before `tx.Commit`; it does not roll back verification failures, so failed evidence can coexist with a successfully altered scratch database.
- **chunk-0124.md:3** — `src/internal/backupverify/verifier.go:150-151`, `src/internal/database/backup/run_repo.go:150-153`, `src/internal/models/backup/backup.go:145-160`: Verification freshness and `backup_at` use `BackupRun.CreatedAt` (when the run was queued), not `CompletedAt` (when the artifact was finished). A backup can remain queued/running for a substantial interval; the verifier then rejects or reports it as stale based on the queue time, and even a newly completed artifact gets an age belonging to the run's start. `BackupRun` already persists `CompletedAt`, and completion sets it in `Complete`; `VerifyLatest` has the canonical artifact timestamp available but ignores it, so freshness has the wrong clock domain.
- **chunk-0124.md:4** — `src/internal/backupverify/verifier.go:180`, `src/internal/backup/processor.go:291-306`, `src/internal/backup/processor.go:122-135`, `src/internal/models/backup/backup.go:117-145`: `RestoreBackup` decides whether to gzip-decompress solely from `run.FileName` ending in `.gz`; it does not inspect the artifact or `FilePath`. A valid gzip backup copied under a missing, renamed, or stale `FileName` is returned to JSON parsing as compressed bytes, while the matching `.json.gz` `FilePath` is ignored. The writer couples compression to both `FileName` and `FilePath` when it uploads, but `RestoreBackup`'s restore contract relies on only one piece of persisted metadata; metadata loss or provider/API row rewrites can make byte-identical artifacts restoreable or fail depending on the filename pointer.
- **chunk-0124.md:5** — `src/internal/backupverify/verifier.go:180`, `src/internal/backupverify/verifier.go:155-178`, `src/internal/models/backup/backup.go:117-145`, `src/internal/api/backup/restore_handler.go:328-330`, `src/internal/api/backup/restore_handler.go:365-370`: The verifier restores using the provider and credentials from the **current** config (`cfg.Provider`, `cfg.ProviderConfig`) instead of the provider recorded on the successful run (`run.Provider`). Restore handlers use `run.Provider` but the config for credentials. If a backup config changes provider after an artifact was created, verification downloads a provider-relative `run.FilePath` through the wrong provider/current configuration and may verify the wrong or a non-existent object. `BackupRun.Provider` is explicitly persisted at backup creation, while the verifier has the same config lookup; the two provider sources are not synchronized if configuration is edited later, producing artifact/config drift.
- **chunk-0124.md:6** — `src/internal/backupverify/verifier_test.go:31-57`, `src/internal/backupverify/verifier_test.go:64-94`, `src/internal/backupverify/verify_latest_test.go:62-144`: Several verifier tests do not exercise the branch named by the test: `TestVerifyLatest_NoSuccessfulBackup` and the old-run test supply a nil processor, so `VerifyLatest` short-circuits at `"verifier not configured"` before looking up the run; the stale, latest-run, no-backup, and similar cases are also conflated with the nil dependency setup in separate tests. A suite can pass while `LatestSuccessful`, staleness, no-success, and lookup behavior are unverified, because the precondition guard masks the intended result. Test comments in `verifier_test.go` acknowledge this construction shortcut instead of injecting an active processor/repository state, weakening the verification contract.
- **chunk-0124.md:7** — `src/cmd/backup-verify/main.go:111-125`, `src/cmd/backup-restore-drill/main.go:106-118`, `src/internal/backupverify/verifier.go:82-89`: With no override, the backup-verify command passes no critical-table list so `NewVerifier` defaults to only `"vehicles"`; the restore-drill command defaults to `{"vehicles","drives","charging_sessions"}`; the package comments also advertise all three as invariants. The same verifier name has materially different default completeness depending on entry point, so a bare backup verification can pass a backup that the restore drill considers missing critical tables, and operators receive inconsistent default drill contracts.
- **chunk-0125.md:3** — `src/internal/cache/cache.go:56-60`, `src/internal/cache/mem.go:32-40`, `src/internal/cache/cache_test.go:11-145`: `Store.Get` does not implement the documented Redis-to-memory fallback when Redis bytes are present but cannot be unmarshaled (wrong JSON shape/type or corrupt bytes): it returns the direct result of `json.Unmarshal` and never calls `mem.Get`. The same Store normally writes valid copies to both Redis and memory, so a degraded/corrupted Redis value suppresses an otherwise usable local value even though Redis configuration alone is not the only cache source. `cache_test.go` covers missing Redis keys, outages, and successful values but has no corrupt-value or type-mismatch Redis case, so this fallback failure is unguarded.
- **chunk-0125.md:4** — `src/internal/cache/cache.go:86-97`, `src/internal/cache/cache_test.go:118-137`: `Store.Invalidate` has no way to distinguish a successful Redis invalidation from a scan/delete failure; it only scans `pattern+"*"` and calls `rdb.Del`, but discards iterator and delete errors before always invalidating memory. A transient Redis command failure can leave stale Redis entries while the caller receives no error (the method has no return value), and later `Get` can still return the stale Redis value because Redis has precedence over memory. The existing memory invalidation test proves only the local branch; it does not exercise Redis scan/delete errors or assert that Redis entries disappear, leaving stale distributed cache behavior unverified.
- **chunk-0127.md:3** — `src/internal/config/config.go:671-710`, `src/internal/config/config_test.go:216-235`: `Load` exposes an error return but performs no validation, while the shared integer/duration parsers accept values that are negative or otherwise unusable for runtime configuration. For example, `DATABASE_MAX_CONNS=-1`, `MIN_CONNS=-1`, port `0/-1`, telemetry batch/window values, and timeout/retention durations can enter the returned `Config`; the database pool, HTTP listeners, MQTT batching, and cleanup paths receive those values without the invalid configuration being rejected. There is no bounded validation counterpart in this package, although the deprecated `src/internal/platform/config/config.go` retains explicit checks for server ports, database bounds, auth, and Redis ports. The active package therefore has the same environment-loading responsibility but silently accepts malformed/unsafe values instead of falling back or failing startup.
- **chunk-0128.md:5** — `src/internal/crypto/crypto.go:110-132`, `src/internal/database/auth/token_repo.go:39-56`: The `EncryptIfEnabled` and `DecryptIfEnabled` wrappers silently convert cryptographic failures into data-flow fallbacks: any encryption error returns plaintext, while any decryption error (including a wrong key, truncated data, corruption, or authentication failure) returns the original ciphertext. `TokenRepo` relies on these wrappers without checking success, so a storage or key problem can persist/return unusable secrets instead of failing the operation visibly. The unit tests cover only nil encryptors and successful round trips, not encryption failures or wrong-key legacy ambiguity. The same fallback contract appears in both wrapper directions: encryption silently falls forward to plaintext, decryption silently falls back to its input, and both are consumed by the same token repository. This also defeats the caller's ability to distinguish legacy plaintext from authenticated encrypted data or a corrupted/wrong-key ciphertext.
- **chunk-0128.md:6** — `src/internal/crypto/totp_secret.go:192-228`, `src/internal/api/totp/handler.go:496-503,677-684`: `HashBackupCodeHex` is described as a thin re-export of the canonical backup-code hash, but it does not normalize the input and merely hashes the raw string with SHA-256. Production enrollment and verification use `crypto.HashBackupCode`, which strips whitespace, dashes, and underscores and uppercases the remaining ASCII characters before hashing. Calling `HashBackupCodeHex("abcd-efgh-jkmn-pqrs")` therefore produces a different value from actual verification, despite the helper promising stable/hash-equivalent behavior. The canonical hash and the handler-local helper advertise the same backup-code identity contract but implement different normalization, so router/test wiring that substitutes the local helper can generate or consume codes that the real repository will never match.
- **chunk-0129.md:3** — `src/internal/database/activity/repo.go:115-151`, `src/internal/database/actioncenter/source_repo.go:38-54`, `src/internal/database/actioncenter/source_repo_test.go:64-163`: The unified activity timeline misattributes a scoped alert notification when the rule is fleet-wide (`all_vehicles=true`) or otherwise has no single vehicle. The alert branch evaluates `WHEN $1 IS NOT NULL AND ar.id IS NOT NULL THEN $1`, before the `ar.id IS NULL OR ar.all_vehicles` branch, so `vehicle_id` becomes the requested vehicle instead of the documented `NULL` fleet/multi-vehicle attribution. The separately implemented Action Center active-alert query also omits this scoping contract: it filters only `ar.vehicle_id = $2`, but never follows `alert_rule_vehicles` or includes `ar.all_vehicles`, so legitimate targeted/fleet alerts are excluded or misclassified relative to the activity feed. Both feeds should share one alert-target projection/filter contract. The activity source comment explicitly says scoped selection is the projected vehicle, but also says fleet/all-vehicle rules are `NULL`; the SQL violates that latter rule whenever a scoped vehicle is supplied. This can assign a global incident to a specific vehicle and produce inconsistent Action Center evidence.
- **chunk-0129.md:4** — `src/internal/database/admin/places_cache_repo.go:42-56`, `src/internal/database/geofence/repo.go:221-229`, `src/internal/database/worker/unit_drift_validator.go:655-662` (equivalent meter haversine copy): Place-cache proximity is documented and implemented as Haversine, but the SQL uses a spherical-law-of-cosines `acos(...)` expression. It clamps only values above 1 before calling `acos`, so numeric drift below `-1` or boundary-rounding can produce a PostgreSQL domain error, while a canonical Haversine implementation here remains numerically stable with `atan2(sqrt(a), sqrt(1-a))`. At the 50 m `Upsert` and caller-provided radius boundaries, the two formulas can therefore disagree by a few centimetres and/or fail where the shared geofence implementation succeeds. The canonical geofence packages already use Haversine with `earthRadius=6371000`; replacing this one SQL implementation with parameterized Haversine math must preserve same-domain null/zero behavior and avoid a second unstable formula.
- **chunk-0129.md:5** — `src/internal/database/admin/places_cache_repo.go:76-104`, `src/migrations/000024_places_cache.up.sql:20-22`: `Upsert` performs a separate `FindNearby` SELECT followed by an INSERT, but `places_cache` has only a non-unique `(latitude, longitude)` coordinate index. Two concurrent upserts for the same 50 m bucket can both observe no row and insert duplicates; the subsequent cache reads return whichever row `last_used_at DESC` wins rather than a single authoritative hit-count entry. The read-modify-write sequence is cross-file and spans application SQL plus the migration, with no database uniqueness constraint for its claimed 50 m semantic. A retry/transaction around the lookup plus uniqueness or a PostgreSQL advisory lock/unique normalized key is needed to make hit counts and lookup deterministic.
- **chunk-0130.md:5** — `src/internal/database/admin/dashboard_layout_repo.go:88-105,106-135`, `src/internal/database/admin/saved_views_repo.go:106-139,152-204`, `src/migrations/000156_dashboard_layouts.up.sql:23-39`: Dashboard layouts have no uniqueness/deduplication enforcement for defaults: `Create` inserts a caller-supplied `is_default=true` directly, and `Update` can mark any layout default without clearing peer rows. The migration indexes defaults but is not unique, unlike `saved_views`, which atomically clears peer defaults before claiming the slot. Two dashboard layouts can therefore be reported as default for the same `(user_id, vehicle_id)` scope. `SetDefault` correctly clears peers, but bypass paths can create divergent state; dashboard listing orders equal-default rows by name/id and switcher behavior becomes ambiguous.
- **chunk-0130.md:6** — `src/internal/database/advancedintelligence/source_repository.go:691-757`, `src/internal/database/advancedintelligence/source_repository_cost_test.go:42-70`, `src/internal/database/advancedintelligence/source_repository.go:470-525`: The advanced-intelligence TCO query converts home/public charging totals with `ROUND(SUM(cost_decimal) * 100)::bigint`, but `cost_decimal` is a currency-denominated major-unit value whose minor-unit scale is currency-specific (for example USD 100, JPY 1, KWD 1000, CLF 10000). That SQL hardcodes cents and silently misreports e.g. JPY and KWD while the adjacent `ListChargingEvidence` path already performs exact, currency-aware denomination via `chargingCostMinor`. A mixed-currency fleet query can additionally filter by `$2`, so one query may be currency-safe nominally, but the scale remains wrong for any requested currency whose exponent is not 2. Maintenance costs are already stored as minor-unit `cost_minor`, further mixing units in the same evidence object.
- **chunk-0130.md:7** — `src/internal/database/ai/call_log_repo.go:156-210`, `src/internal/ai/features/registry.go:3895-3908`, `src/migrations/000203_ai_call_log.up.sql:124-139`: `AICallLogRepo.Insert` does not implement the feature-registry validation documented by its own comments, migration commentary, and `features.IsKnown`. It accepts every non-empty string and, for empty feature IDs, mutates a missing value to `""` and inserts it. Unknown/empty IDs therefore corrupt the canonical per-feature usage breakdown despite the schema/comment contract that every row must match a registered feature. The registry is the authoritative canonical export (`Registry`, `IsKnown`, `Get`), and no validation prevents typoed audit records. Usage totals may show bogus feature buckets and empty IDs.
- **chunk-0130.md:8** — `src/internal/database/ai/call_log_repo.go:225-240,250-277`, `src/internal/database/ai/call_log_repo.go:87-106`, `src/internal/database/ai/call_log_repo.go:299-326`: `Today` computes its UTC day boundary as `date_trunc('day', now() AT TIME ZONE 'UTC')` and compares it directly to a `timestamptz`. Unlike the other AI usage queries, this boundary is session-timezone-dependent after PostgreSQL casts the `timestamp` boundary back to `timestamptz`; a database session configured outside UTC can make “today” start on the wrong UTC instant. `DailySpend` explicitly receives UTC bounds and passes `.UTC()` to SQL; `Recent`/`ByFeature` use absolute timestamps. Only `Today` embeds an implicit timezone conversion in the boundary, causing inconsistent windows when `TimeZone` differs.
- **chunk-0133.md:3** — `src/internal/database/automation/step_repo.go:49-66`, `src/migrations/000142_baseline_typed.up.sql:817-823`, `src/migrations/_baseline_source/14-automations.sql:35-41`: `AutomationStepRepo.UpdateOrder` updates `step_order` values one row at a time while the `(automation_id, step_order)` unique constraint is enforced immediately. A two-step swap such as moving step 1 to order 2 first attempts to give a row an order already occupied by step 2, violating the unique constraint before the later update can move step 2 away; the method therefore cannot safely perform the ordinary reorder transaction it documents. The repository code claims transactional batching, but both the baseline migration and its source baseline define an immediate unique key on `automation_id, step_order`, so in-place swaps can fail under valid inputs.
- **chunk-0133.md:4** — `src/internal/database/automation/variable_repo.go:23-42`, `src/internal/automation/action/variable.go:91-116`, `src/migrations/000109_add_automations.up.sql:89-94`: Cross-automation variables are persisted with a globally unique key but are also passed a vehicle scope by `SetVariableExecutor`; `AutomationVariableRepo.Get` ignores vehicle scope, and `Set` uses `ON CONFLICT (key)` to overwrite both the value and `vehicle_id`. Two vehicles using the same variable key will therefore share or overwrite each other's state instead of retaining the vehicle-specific state represented by the model's `VehicleID` field. The action executor supplies a non-nil `vehicleID`, the model distinguishes global from vehicle-scoped variables, but the repository lookup/upsert key and migration uniqueness are global-only; this is a data-isolation edge for per-vehicle automations.
- **chunk-0133.md:5** — `src/internal/database/cache.go:76-80`, `src/internal/database/cache_test.go:139-154`: `Cache.Close` closes `done` directly and is not idempotent. Calling `Close` more than once panics with `close of closed channel`, so repeated shutdown paths, deferred cleanup plus explicit cleanup, or lifecycle teardown can crash the process instead of degrading safely. The test suite only exercises cleanup once through `defer`; it does not cover two or repeated calls, leaving the public close operation vulnerable to a duplicate close.
- **chunk-0133.md:6** — `src/internal/api/chargeautopilot/compute.go:157-160`, `src/internal/api/chargeautopilot/compute.go:21-23`, `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/handler.go:190-223`, `src/internal/api/chargeplanner/dtos.go:11-17`, `src/internal/database/charging/autopilot_repo.go:20-23`: Smart Charging Autopilot preview calculates charging rate from a fixed `240.0 V` assumption even though the neighboring charge planner accepts an explicit charger voltage and defaults it to 240 V. The Autopilot profile schema and repository expose no voltage field, so 120 V, 208 V, or other installation configurations are silently treated as 240 V; required charge hours, feasibility, start windows, and charge costs can all be materially wrong. The canonical charge planner computes `charger_voltage * max_amps / 1000`; Autopilot uses only fixed voltage, while its persisted profile cannot represent a real charger voltage, creating behavior divergence for residential installations below 240 V.
- **chunk-0134.md:5** — `src/internal/database/charging/plan_repo.go:13-29,42-51`, `src/internal/database/charging/autopilot_repo.go:10-23,38-79`, `src/internal/api/chargeautopilot/compute.go:20-21,157`, `src/internal/api/nextcharge/decide.go:104`: Charging plans and autopilot configuration still use legacy KWh-named fields and carry KWh through Go domain/API computation instead of the SI migration contract. `ChargePlan.EstimatedKWh`, `AutopilotProfile.BatteryCapacityKWh`, and derived planner/autopilot calculations preserve `_kwh`/`KWh` names even though the active Phase-48 rule requires SI canonical fields (`Wh`) and display-only conversion. This is a live write/read contract mismatch risk and leaves legacy unit suffixes in newly exercised code paths. The same legacy unit appears in the charge-plan repository and the autopilot profile repository, with two independent schedulers computing by multiplying or dividing it. They should share SI Wh/canonical capacity semantics; otherwise persisted plans and profile values can drift or be interpreted under different units.
- **chunk-0134.md:6** — `src/internal/database/database.go:29,89`, `src/internal/database/circuit_breaker.go:30-63`, `src/internal/api/health.go:87-88,348-349`: The database write circuit breaker is constructed and published as a health/metrics state, but no database write path calls `WriteBreaker.Execute`; a repository-wide search finds only construction and health exposure. It therefore remains permanently closed regardless of write failures, while health checks can misleadingly report normal database write resilience and operators receive no fast-fail behavior. The retry helper exists separately in `src/internal/database/retry.go`, but it is also unconnected to database write operations; together, the configured breaker/retry machinery has no enforcement point in the write repositories.
- **chunk-0134.md:7** — `src/internal/database/datarepair/anomaly_scan.go:32-73`, `src/internal/database/datarepair/anomaly_scan_test.go:44-48`: `ListSessionAnomalies` marks `Truncated=true` whenever any one source returns exactly the effective SQL limit, even if that source contains no anomalies or returns fewer than the limit after classification. For example, `driveAnomalyRowsQuery` can return exactly 500 candidate rows while most do not actually satisfy the anomaly rules, and `anySourceAtLimit` still reports truncation. The caller can therefore display a false “results truncated” warning and unnecessarily narrow a scan. The accompanying `TestAnySourceAtLimitReportsPotentialSQLTruncation` pins this potential/proactive behavior instead of the stated actual truncation contract in `AnomalyScanResult`: it has evidence that `Truncated` means raw detection count exceeded the limit, not that a source merely filled a candidate LIMIT.
- **chunk-0134.md:8** — `src/internal/database/datarepair/anomaly_scan.go:220-278`, `src/internal/database/datarepair/anomaly_scan_test.go:144-177`: The same-lookback overlap queries combine a half-open pair lookback with candidate filters that require both session ends to be `>= since`, while requiring at least one start to be `>= since`. A long-session overlap beginning before `since` and both ending at or after `since` is excluded unless one start moved inside the window, so anomalies are not detected merely because the overlap/intersection occurs inside the lookback. This is inconsistent with the method's “bounded scan starting at since” contract and can suppress genuine cross-kind and same-kind anomalies. The overlap detector has duplicate SQL for drive/charging pairs plus a cross-kind query, so each form has the same window-boundary risk. Tests validate placeholders and canonicalization but do not pin a pair whose two starts predate `since` and whose intersection ends/starts within it.
- **chunk-0135.md:3** — `src/internal/database/drive/diagnostic_repo.go:179-205`, `src/internal/tesla/router/writers/signal_log_writer.go:620-645`, `src/internal/tesla/router/writers/signal_log_writer_test.go:410-425`: Enum diagnostic values are never rendered: `renderTypedValue` handles `valueKind == 7` only through `strValue`, but enum-valued atoms are classified into `signalLogKindEnum` and written exclusively to `int_value`. The diagnostic query also scans `int_value`, but the renderer's enum branch ignores that scanned integer. Fields such as Gear, ChargeState, and SentryMode therefore produce an empty display value instead of their enum value in the per-drive diagnostics. The same writer/test pair pins enums as `kind=7` with an `int64` integer column and verifies no string column is populated; the renderer contradicts that storage contract by returning only `str_value`.
- **chunk-0135.md:4** — `src/internal/database/drive/place_label_migration_test.go:34-49`, `src/internal/database/drive/repo.go:598-607`, `src/migrations/000226_drive_place_label_version.up.sql:16-17`, `src/migrations/000227_drive_endpoint_repair.up.sql:24-40`: The migration test compares `SET DEFAULT` to the live `PlaceLabelVersion`, now revision 3, but migration 000226 intentionally sets the default to 2 and migration 000227 raises it to 3. As written, `TestPlaceLabelMigrationBackfillsExistingRowsAsStale` will fail even when migration 000226 is correct, and it incorrectly couples a historical migration to the latest revision. Migration 226 is baseline revision 2; migration 227 performs the revision-3 requeue/default/index upgrade. A historical migration test must pin 2 (and separately pin 227's revision 3), not use a mutable current revision constant.
- **chunk-0136.md:5** — `src/internal/database/export/scheduled_repo.go:602`, `src/internal/export/scheduler.go:64-66,182`, `src/internal/export/scheduler_test.go:47`: The concrete scheduled-export repository implements `DueBefore(ctx, cutoff)` without a limit, while the scheduler's `SchedulerStore` contract requires `DueBefore(ctx, cutoff, limit)`; this implementation signature mismatch will prevent assigning the concrete repo as the production scheduler store. The repository comment also claims worker locking, but its SQL is a plain unbounded `SELECT`; it has neither `LIMIT` nor `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, and `scheduler.Start` can process a row more than once across replicas or ticks because it does not atomically claim it before processing. The migration comment promises `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED`, while `scheduled_repo.go` omits both locking and batching and the interface/test contract adds a limit, showing three divergent contracts for the same due-row operation.
- **chunk-0136.md:6** — `src/internal/database/export/repo.go:27-44`, `src/internal/api/backup/handler.go:38-44`, `src/internal/export/processor.go:556-563`, `src/migrations/000180_drop_legacy_telemetry.up.sql:45-55`: The account and backup export allowlists still advertise legacy tables that migration `000180` deliberately drops, including `vampire_drain_events`, and also include `daily_mileage`, `vehicle_states`, and `visited_locations` after the legacy telemetry rewrite. Account export attempts each table and silently logs/skips failures; backup export likewise omits them from data after query errors; both can therefore report success with incomplete archives while `manifest`/backup output contains no honest gap for the account table list. Three independent export allowlists duplicate the stale names and the drop migration explicitly removes the same tables, so this is drift affecting account export, admin backup, and worker backup processing rather than an intentional compatibility snapshot.
- **chunk-0136.md:7** — `src/internal/database/export/job_repo.go:53-82`: `ExportJobRepo.List` iterates result rows but never checks `rows.Err()` after the loop. A mid-stream pgx iteration/row-decoding failure can return a partial job slice with `nil` error, making the exports API claim success and report partial results to callers. The neighboring repository methods in this package generally handle `rows.Err()` after loops; this method's missing terminal check bypasses that package convention for exactly the list path used by `ListJobs`.
- **chunk-0137.md:5** — `src/internal/database/geofence/repo_bulk.go:40-55`, `src/internal/api/geofence/bulk_handler.go:49-63`, `src/internal/api/geofence/handler.go:359-382`, `src/internal/database/geofence/repo_archive.go:71-84`: The geofence bulk-delete endpoint hard-deletes every existing ID returned by `FilterExistingIDs`; `BulkDelete` executes one unguarded `DELETE FROM geofences WHERE id = ANY($1)`. The single-geofence `Delete` path first calls `HasChargingHistory` and refuses places referenced by charging sessions, rates, or drive endpoints, but the bulk path performs no equivalent history check. A bulk request can therefore orphan historical `charging_sessions.geofence_id`, `geofence_rates.geofence_id`, and drive start/end geofence IDs even though the same repository deliberately has no foreign keys for telemetry-hot-path integrity. This bypasses the same-place archive/hard-delete contract implemented by `Delete` plus `HasChargingHistory`; the archive route exists specifically to preserve historical place resolution, but bulk delete silently contradicts it.
- **chunk-0137.md:6** — `src/internal/database/fleetops/scheduling_repo.go:178-226`, `src/internal/database/fleetops/scheduling_repo.go:63-75`, `src/internal/database/fleetops/scheduling_repo.go:315-333`: The assignment mutation reservation checks are containment checks rather than overlap checks. In `DeleteAssignment`, a finite assignment only detects reservations where both `r.starts_at >= assignment.starts_at` and `r.ends_at <= assignment.ends_at`; a reservation that overlaps the assignment start/end but is not wholly contained can be left referring to a driver after the assignment is deleted (and open-ended assignments disable the end check entirely). `UpdateAssignment` repeats the same kind of check against the old interval, then separately validates `ensureDriverAssigned` only for the new assignment. Together these queries can miss affected overlapping reservations and corrupt the scheduling invariant instead of detecting overlap/dependency consistently. `ensureDriverAssigned` uses interval coverage (`a.starts_at <= reservation.starts_at` and assignment end is null or `assignment.ends_at >= reservation.ends_at`), but mutation paths try a different containment/edge test (`r.starts_at >= assignment.starts_at` and optional `r.ends_at <= assignment.ends_at`). The implementation therefore disagrees for reservations that begin before, or extend beyond, an assignment interval.
- **chunk-0138.md:3** — `src/internal/api/geofence/bulk_handler.go:14-19,53-62`, `src/internal/database/geofence/repo_bulk.go:34-56`, `src/internal/database/geofence/repo_archive.go:63-82`, `src/internal/api/geofence/handler.go:358-393`: The geofence bulk-delete endpoint hard-deletes every existing ID with `BulkDelete`, bypassing the single-delete historical-integrity guard. `HasChargingHistory` explicitly redirects deletes with charging-session, rate, or drive references to archive so those no-FK provenance IDs keep resolving, but bulk deletion performs no equivalent check and only `DELETE FROM geofences WHERE id = ANY($1)`; it therefore silently orphans `geofence_id`, `rate_id`, `start_geofence_id`, and `end_geofence_id` and permits bulk deletion of places that the API correctly rejects singly. The repository is cross-file with the single-delete guard and the migration that intentionally has no FKs on telemetry attribution; bulk deletion reproduces the exact historical-loss risk by skipping that guard and never returns per-ID protection failures.
- **chunk-0138.md:4** — `src/internal/database/notification/push_subscriptions_repo.go:37-50`, `src/migrations/000165_push_subscriptions.up.sql:26-45`: `PushSubscriptionsRepo.Upsert` documents and partially models refresh-on-resubscribe, but its `ON CONFLICT` clause updates neither `last_used_at` nor any creation timestamp; it returns the pre-existing `last_used_at` unchanged while replacing the keys/user-agent. Therefore a browser that successfully re-submits or rotates keys can remain “last used” at an old timestamp indefinitely (until another delivery calls `Touch`), despite upsert success, and can cause stale-device diagnostics and cleanup decisions to misrepresent actual availability. The migration's schema and index cover one row per user/endpoint and the comment promises refresh semantics, but the upsert's conflicting values deliberately set `last_used_at=NULL` and its conflict update omits it; only successful push delivery currently calls `Touch`, so subscription refresh alone has no clock update.
- **chunk-0139.md:3** — `src/internal/database/observability/status_incidents_repo.go:411-463`, `src/internal/api/status/incidents.go:130-168`, `src/internal/database/observability/status_incidents_repo_test.go:580-610`: `Patch` recognizes `"resolved"` only when the separate `Resolved` boolean is true. If a caller sets `IncidentPatch.Status` to `resolved` without also setting `Resolved`, the update persists status `"resolved"` but leaves `ResolvedAt` nil. The append-update implementation correctly stamps resolution on status transitions (`src/internal/database/observability/status_incidents_repo.go:479-510`), so the two write paths disagree. The PATCH payload exposes `status` independently and the handler forwards it verbatim; the SQL contract cannot represent an incident that is resolved in status but has no resolution timestamp, while dashboard/post-mortem logic uses `ResolvedAt` as the resolution time.
- **chunk-0139.md:4** — `src/internal/database/notification/report.go:52-68`, `src/internal/database/notification/report.go:118-132`, `src/internal/database/notification/report_test.go:10-23`: Trigger attribution is grouped by `trigger_id`, but the SQL has no `trigger_id IS NOT NULL` predicate in the triggered-event CTE. All historical triggered rows with a NULL producer UUID collapse into one `NULL` group in `triggers`, so `COUNT(*)` undercounts the true number of firings instead of preserving one trigger per canonical event. The report test explicitly documents the producer UUID as the anti-double-count key and warns that legacy rows must not be counted as distinct triggers, but it only protects delivery rows with `WHERE trigger_id IS NULL`; the triggered CTE has the opposite risk for unknown historical attribution and can merge multiple firings into one.
- **chunk-0139.md:5** — `src/internal/database/notification/schedule_repo.go:25-47`, `src/internal/database/notification/schedule_repo.go:50-77`, `src/internal/database/notification/schedule_repo.go:97-119`, `src/internal/database/notification/schedule_repo.go:127-147`: Four notification repository list methods iterate `pgx.Rows` without checking `rows.Err()` after scanning: schedule `List`, schedule `GetDue`, preference `GetByChannel`, and metric `GetByChannel`. A late iteration or connection error is therefore converted into a partial or empty success response instead of an error. Repeated list implementations in one repository exhibit the same database read contract; `ListSessions`, `ListChargePoints`, and other repository methods in this snapshot return row iteration errors, so callers lose the established failure signal here.
- **chunk-0139.md:6** — `src/internal/database/ocpp/store.go:198-203`, `src/internal/database/ocpp/store_test.go:4-12`: `clamp` slices a Go string at an arbitrary byte offset. For non-ASCII charger IDs, vendor/model names, reasons, or status text within two bytes of a length limit, the result can end with a partial UTF-8 rune, which PostgreSQL text/JSON paths reject instead of storing a valid clamped string. The unit test covers only ASCII input, so it does not pin the UTF-8 boundary. The same class of truncation bug must back off to a rune boundary as the snapshot's incident text helper does elsewhere.
- **chunk-0139.md:7** — `src/internal/database/ocpp/store.go:91-105`, `src/internal/database/ocpp/store_test.go:27-35`: `strconv.ParseFloat` accepts IEEE values such as `NaN`, `+Inf`, and `-Inf`, but `RecordMeterValues` sends every value accepted by this parser into a PostgreSQL batch without a finite-value check. Poisonous or malformed meter samples can fail the entire batch with a database error or contaminate telemetry values, contrary to the method's comment that non-numeric samples are skipped. The parser test verifies RFC3339 fallback but does not test non-finite meter values; the in-memory/dispatcher semantics treat numeric parsing and storage as distinct, and NaN/Inf are not ordinary OCPP numeric readings.
- **chunk-0140.md:5** — `src/internal/database/ownershipintel/source_repository.go:233-282`, `src/internal/database/ownershipintel/source_repository.go:287-308`: `DatasetStats` reports inflated on-disk bytes for every TimescaleDB hypertable. It first calls `pg_total_relation_size(c.oid)` on the hypertable relation and, if `IsHypertable` is true, then adds the sum of each chunk's `pg_total_relation_size` again. A retention-governance consumer sees the same data as both parent-relation bytes and chunk bytes, so disk-pressure and reclaim estimates are overstated and can trigger incorrect retention decisions. The same package's `ListDrives`/`DatasetExpiry` paths rely on this catalog size for governance (`src/internal/database/ownershipintel/source_repository.go:287-308`, `src/internal/app/ownershipintelsvc/governance.go:239-263`), but the established chunk-byte implementation in `src/internal/database/observability/hypertable_metrics_repo.go:159-168` sums `pg_total_relation_size` only over `timescaledb_information.chunks` and does not add a parent/hypertable size. `DatasetStats` is the only copy adding both totals.
- **chunk-0140.md:6** — `src/internal/notification/quiet_hours.go:134-168`, `src/internal/notification/quiet_hours_test.go:225-264`: `NextWindowEndAt` constructs the next wall-clock end with `time.Date` but does not account for whether that local wall-clock time exists or is ambiguous during a DST transition. For the same-day branch, or the next-day boundary of a wrapped window, Go can normalize a nonexistent spring-forward end (such as 02:30) or choose an offset for an ambiguous fall-back end, yielding a replay/deferred-notification end instant that differs from the intended window boundary. Active-window matching in `src/internal/notification/quiet_hours.go:88-132` intentionally evaluates wall-clock minutes in the configured zone, so matching can say a window is active while `NextWindowEndAt` derives the wrong absolute return time; the tests at `src/internal/notification/quiet_hours_test.go:225-264` cover ordinary UTC/local dates only and pin neither DST gap nor overlap behavior. Deferred notifications replay and delivery timing therefore drift at exactly the DST edge.
- **chunk-0141.md:5** — `src/internal/database/serviceintelligence/observation_repo.go:17-73`, `src/internal/database/serviceintelligence/observation_repo_test.go:8-33`, `src/internal/api/serviceintelligence/service.go:93-102`: `recentObservationsQuery` treats every non-NULL `float_value`/`int_value` in `signal_log` as numeric telemetry. It has no value-kind or field allowlist, so enum and boolean integer encodings (for example Gear, ChargeState, or SentryMode) are included in `AVG`/`STDDEV_SAMP` and may be promoted as anomalous symptoms. The service then ranks these synthetic numeric deviations as observed evidence and communication/recall symptoms. The query and its test cover only the table/column shape and explicitly forbid snapshot tables; no test pins exclusion of enum/boolean kinds or shows that `int_value` integer codes belong to numeric signals. An enum sample cannot be compared with its numeric baseline without a signal/value-kind contract, so this can produce false service-intelligence findings.
